AI Usage and Authorship Statement
Portions of this report were developed using AI-assisted content generation tools. Human–AI
collaboration was employed to synthesize findings, organize technical content, and assist in
drafting sections of the document. All AI-generated content was subjected to internal review,
verification, and editing by the project team to ensure factual accuracy, technical correctness,
and alignment with the project’s objectives. The final report reflects a fully human-supervised
authorship process, integrating AI-assisted drafting with domain expertise and rigorous quality
control.

Executive Summary

This final technical report summarizes a multi-year collaboration between Penn State University and Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL), conducted under Subcontract Nos. 424285
and 587178. The work focused on understanding high strain rate behavior in composite armor
systems, especially Dyneema® , through the development of a miniaturized Kolsky bar system,
integration of advanced diagnostics, and validation of embedded finite element modeling strategies.
A custom 3.16 mm diameter Kolsky bar was designed and validated to perform ultra-high
strain rate testing on materials of interest. The system included features such as optimized pulse
shaping, custom fixturing, and high-bandwidth signal acquisition. Dyneema samples tested using this platform exhibited rate-dependent behavior, and failure modes observed under controlled
laboratory conditions correlated with those from full-scale ballistic impact experiments at LANL.
High-resolution CT imaging enabled non-destructive internal inspection of specimens, supporting comparison between experimental outcomes and computational predictions.
Computationally, the project developed an embedded element modeling framework capable
of capturing fiber orientation effects in Dyneema and similar composites. The embedded fiber
method was validated through dynamic simulation of Kolsky bar tests and impact loading scenarios, with particular attention to volume correction, anisotropic behavior, and mesh fidelity.
Simulations conducted using both Abaqus/Explicit and the open-source FLagSHyP finite element code demonstrated agreement with experimental data in terms of stress–strain response,
dynamic equilibrium, and predicted failure locations.
The effort also laid the foundation for advanced diagnostics, including full-field optical strain
measurement and high-speed imaging integration. These tools are expected to enhance future
hybrid experimental–computational studies of dynamic deformation in composite materials.
The following peer-reviewed publications and conference proceedings were produced as part
of this LANL-supported project:

2

Introduction

Understanding the high strain rate response of fiber-reinforced polymer composites is critical
for the design of next-generation penetration-resistant armor systems. Among these materials,
Dyneema® , an ultra-high molecular weight polyethylene (UHMWPE) laminate, offers outstanding ballistic performance due to its high strength-to-weight ratio and energy dissipation capacity.
However, accurate mechanical characterization of Dyneema and similar composites under dynamic loading has remained a persistent challenge due to limitations in both testing infrastructure
and computational modeling approaches.
To address these gaps, this multi-year collaboration between Penn State University and Los
Alamos National Laboratory (LANL) was initiated with two overarching goals: (1) to design
and validate a miniaturized Kolsky bar system capable of testing small-diameter polymer and
composite samples at high strain rates, and (2) to develop and verify advanced computational
tools for modeling the response of anisotropic, fiber-reinforced materials under dynamic loading. Traditional Kolsky bar (also known as split-Hopkinson pressure bar) systems, while wellestablished for characterizing metals and ceramics, reveal different challenges when applied
to fiber-reinforced polymer composites. Unlike homogeneous, isotropic materials, composites
such as Dyneema® exhibit highly anisotropic, layered microstructures that influence stress wave
propagation, failure mechanisms, and data interpretation. These features make them scientifically interesting but also more difficult to test in controlled laboratory settings, particularly at high
strain rates. While ballistic testing has historically been used to study Dyneema’s performance,
it lacks repeatability and controlled loading conditions. In contrast, a portable miniaturized Kolsky bar enables repeatable, high-strain-rate testing (on the order of 103 –104 s−1 ) using smalldiameter specimens (2–3 mm), providing valuable insight into rate-dependent behavior under
well-controlled boundary conditions.
The second axis of the project focused on high-fidelity modeling of fiber-reinforced materials
using embedded element finite element methods. Traditional smeared-property approaches often fail to capture mesoscale mechanisms such as fiber breakage, interfacial delamination, and
direction-dependent stress wave propagation. By embedding truss or beam elements aligned
with the fiber directions within a solid host mesh, the project enabled accurate representation
of orthotropic behavior and facilitated direct comparison with CT-imaged post-mortem data from
experiments. In combination, these experimental and computational efforts represent a significant step toward a fully integrated test-and-modeling framework for soft armor systems. The
validated mini Kolsky bar platform developed in this work offers a replicable, scalable solution
for high-rate polymer testing, while the embedded element modeling framework supports design
optimization and surrogate modeling across a range of impact conditions. This report documents
the complete technical arc of the project, including system design, testing protocols, modeling
innovations, Dyneema application studies, and dissemination of results through peer-reviewed
publications and LANL collaborations. For completeness, all peer-reviewed publications resulting from this project are also cited here: [1–8]. Additional background and motivation, including
in-depth discussions of Kolsky bar testing (Appendix A.1), Dyneema material response (Appendix A.2), and embedded element modeling techniques (Appendix A.3), can be found in the
accompanying peer-reviewed publications.

1

3

Technical Objectives

The technical objectives of this project evolved across two main subcontract phases — Subcontract No. 424285 (initial development and prototyping) and Subcontract No. 587178 (validation,
dissemination, and application to Dyneema® systems). Across both phases, the overarching objective was to enable validated, high strain rate testing and simulation of polymer and composite
materials using portable experimental platforms and advanced embedded element models.

3.1

Primary Objectives

1. Design and validate a miniaturized Kolsky bar system with sub-5 mm diameter bars for
high strain rate testing of polymers and soft composites.
2. Establish reproducible testing and analysis protocols including sample design, fixture
setup, signal conditioning, pulse shaping, and statistical error bounds.
3. Develop embedded element finite element methods that accurately represent orthotropic,
fiber-reinforced composite behavior without material smearing.
4. Validate embedded models against experimental results from both mini Kolsky bar
tests and full-scale ballistic or quasi-static plate impact experiments.
5. Apply the experimental and computational framework to Dyneema® and evaluate its
dynamic response across strain rates, geometries, and loading modes.
6. Disseminate results through publications and collaborative testing campaigns with
LANL teams, including CT imaging integration and field-deployable testing protocols.

3.2

Subcontract 424285 (2020–2022): Initial Development Phase

• Valibrate a functional miniaturized Kolsky bar system.
• Demonstrate ability to achieve dynamic equilibrium and measure high-rate stress–strain
curves.
• Develop statistical and uncertainty analysis methods tailored for miniature tests.
• Prototype and validate basic embedded element models in FLagSHyP and Abaqus.

3.3

Subcontract 587178 (2023–2025): Validation and Application Phase

• Perform extensive Dyneema® and Aluminum testing and CT scanning at LANL and PSU.
• Evaluate correlation between mini-bar results and ballistic plate failure mechanisms.
• Expand embedded element framework to handle curvature, frictional interfaces, and thermal effects.
• Disseminate workflows, simulation scripts, and results for LANL use and peer-reviewed
publication.

2

4

Approach and Methods

This project employed a tightly coupled experimental–computational strategy to investigate the
high strain rate deformation behavior of Dyneema® and related composite materials used in
penetration-resistant applications. The approach was anchored by the development of a custom
miniature Kolsky bar system capable of delivering controlled, ultra-high-rate loading to specimens of varying materials. Experimental activities included specimen preparation, dynamic
loading, post-mortem characterization using micro-CT and high-speed imaging, and the implementation of optical strain diagnostics. These procedures were supported by rigorous error quantification and signal processing protocols to ensure fidelity and repeatability. Complementing the experimental efforts, finite element models were developed using both commercial (Abaqus/Explicit) and LANL-supported (FLagSHyP) platforms. The computational work focused on simulating wave propagation in miniature bar systems, implementing embedded element methods for fiber-reinforced materials, and validating against both strain data and internal
failure patterns observed via CT imaging. Simulation results were used to interpret test outcomes, refine system design (e.g., bearing alignment and bar materials), and establish dynamic
equilibrium conditions. The following subsections are divided into two major parts: Experimental Methods and Computational Methods. Each part is organized thematically to highlight key
components of the research effort and their contributions to the integrated model–experiment
framework.

4.1

Experimental Methods

The experimental component of this project centered on designing, validating, and deploying a
custom 3.16 mm diameter Kolsky bar platform for high strain rate testing of soft and composite
materials. This miniature system was engineered to enable controlled loading at strain rates
exceeding 103 s−1 , with capabilities for pulse shaping, precise fixturing, and signal acquisition at
high bandwidth. Over the course of the project, experimental protocols were developed and refined to ensure data reliability and system repeatability. These included systematic error injection
and baseline characterization studies, a suite of specimen preparation and cutting techniques,
and the development of post-test imaging workflows using micro-CT scanning and 3D visualization. Statistical approaches were used to assess signal variability and construct confidence
intervals for dynamic measurements. The integration of high-speed imaging and laser-based
diagnostics, though not fully deployed, also formed a critical developmental thrust and will continue to be explored in future work. The subsections that follow provide a detailed account of
each major experimental element, from bar system design and test execution to damage imaging and specimen fabrication, structured to reflect the evolution and scope of the effort.
4.1.1

Kolsky Bar System Configuration and Instrumentation

This project centered on the development, refinement, and validation of a custom miniaturized
split Hopkinson pressure bar (SHPB) at Penn State, designed specifically for high fidelity testing
of polymeric and fiber-reinforced composites such as Dyneema® . Figure 1 compares a traditional full-scale SHPB system (Figure 1a), initially evaluated for this research, with the compact
miniaturized platform (Figure 1b) that ultimately enabled the precise and repeatable experiments
reported throughout this study.

3

Figure 1a shows the larger Kolsky bar system used at the Penn State Applied Research Laboratory. It features thick incident and transmission bars mounted on a steel rail with pneumatic
launch mechanisms and conventional support hardware. While robust and proven for testing
metals and ceramics, this configuration poses challenges for smaller or lower impedance specimens due to limited alignment control, high momentum transfer, and increased fixture mass. In
contrast, Figure 1b presents the Penn State mini Kolsky bar, a system purpose built for high strain
rate testing of small scale, soft, or layered materials. This configuration uses 3.16 mm diameter
hardened steel bars with embedded strain gauges positioned at the incident, reflected, and transmitted wave locations. The bars are mounted on low friction linear bearings with micrometer-level
adjustability, and the platform sits on a vibration-isolated optical breadboard to support precision
alignment and simplified instrumentation. The inset in Figure 1b highlights the specimen region where deformation occurs and shows the gauge layout used to resolve wave propagation.
The system design also takes advantage of a key principle in split Hopkinson pressure bar testing: nominal strain rate is proportional to impact velocity and inversely proportional to specimen
height. As such, miniaturizing the bar and using smaller samples opens access to the higher
strain rates characteristic of ballistic loading, but in a controlled laboratory setting. The Penn
State mini Kolsky bar was enabled control over waveform generation, data repeatability, and
instrumentation integration, supporting the material characterization presented throughout this
report.

Figure 1: Comparison of Kolsky bar platforms used in this research. (a) Traditional full-scale SHPB
system originally investigated for testing metallic materials. (b) Miniaturized Kolsky bar developed for this
project, featuring 3.16 mm diameter bars, embedded gauges, and a modular instrumentation layout. Inset
highlights the specimen region and diagnostic gauge configuration.

4.1.2

Specimen Preparation and Cutting Techniques

Accurate and consistent specimen preparation was essential for the success of high strain rate
experiments conducted using the miniaturized Kolsky bar system. This effort focused primarily
on Dyneema® laminates and surrogate materials such as PMMA, with careful attention to dimensional precision, alignment tolerances, and edge quality—all of which influence dynamic stress
wave propagation and failure behavior.
4

Dyneema Specimen Geometry. The primary material used was Dyneema HB26. Specimens
were prepared in cylindrical or dogbone geometries with target diameters of 3.16 mm to match
the incident and transmission bar dimensions. Thicknesses ranged from 1 to 3 mm, depending
on the desired strain rate and failure mode. Flat end faces were verified under magnification to
ensure planarity and perpendicularity prior to mounting.
Cutting Methods. Multiple cutting methods were explored to evaluate their influence on edge
quality and laminate integrity. Early phases relied on waterjet cutting due to accessibility and geometric control; however, SEM and CT imaging revealed fiber disruption, embedded debris, and
interfacial delamination in many samples. Thermal and laser-based approaches were subsequently investigated in collaboration with the Schuster Lab at the University of Texas at El Paso.
Their focused laser beam system offered high dimensional precision with minimal mechanical
contact, which proved especially beneficial for thin, layered composites. While laser-induced
thermal effects must be carefully managed, this method consistently produced clean edges with
reduced fiber recoil compared to both abrasive and waterjet techniques. Ongoing collaboration
with the Schuster Lab will support broader adoption of this method in future testing campaigns.
Hot knife cutting was also briefly evaluated for its potential to create sealed laminate edges and
reduce fiber fraying. Although this method showed some promise, it required extensive control of
temperature and blade speed to avoid excessive melting or tapered profiles and was ultimately
not adopted for final specimen batches.
Material Orientation and Fiber Layup. To ensure consistent loading behavior, custom alignment templates were used during cutting and handling to preserve fiber orientation. This was
especially important for off-axis layups (e.g., [0/60/120] and [0/45/90/135]), where small misalignments could alter local stiffness and failure pathways. Fiber directionality was maintained
through visual alignment under magnification and tracked via orientation marks during testing.
Specimen Handling and Mounting. Mounting procedures emphasized axial alignment and
minimal preload. Low-pressure compression fixtures were used to seat specimens between
bars, and concentricity was verified using feeler gauges and imaging tools. Alignment marks
were applied to facilitate consistent rotational positioning across repeated tests.
PMMA Surrogates. As a benchmark material, PMMA was machined into cylindrical and rectangular specimens to validate bar operation. These samples were polished and dimensionally
controlled to reduce surface cracking. PMMA’s well-documented behavior under dynamic loading made it a useful standard for equilibrium verification, strain rate calibration, and waveform
fidelity assessments.
Imaging-Driven Refinements. Microscopy and imaging guided the transition to improved cutting methods. Figure 2 illustrates the effect of different techniques on edge quality. SEM and
CT scans revealed significant defects in waterjet-cut specimens, including embedded particles
and irregular fiber terminations. In contrast, focused laser beam cutting produced clean edges
with minimal delamination and high dimensional consistency. These imaging insights supported
a data-driven shift toward thermal-free, non-contact cutting methods that preserve material architecture and support repeatable test outcomes.
5

Figure 2: Specimen preparation diagnostics. (a) SEM images of Dyneema cross-sections after waterjet
cutting, showing fiber disruption and edge roughness. (b) Edge morphology from focused laser beam
cutting, with minimal delamination. (c) CT imaging reveals internal defects introduced by fluid-based
cutting methods, motivating the adoption of precision laser systems.

Specimen preparation was foundational to the success of the high strain rate testing campaign. By combining precise geometries, orientation control, and validated cutting techniques,
the team established a robust and scalable workflow that minimized experimental uncertainty
and supported reliable material response characterization.
4.1.3

Experimental Testing of Dyneema and Other Materials

Once the miniature Kolsky bar platform and sample preparation procedures were validated, a
series of high strain rate experimental campaigns were conducted. These efforts aimed to characterize the dynamic response of Dyneema® and other selected materials under compressive
loading at strain rates on the order of 103 –104 s−1 . Test data generated from these campaigns
formed the backbone for model calibration, failure mode analysis, and signal repeatability assessment throughout the project.
Baseline No-Sample Tests. Before testing any specimens, a set of over 40 no-sample tests
was performed to benchmark the Kolsky bar system’s internal response. These tests allowed
the team to identify and quantify system noise, bar-bar alignment issues, pulse dispersion, and
fixture-induced friction effects. These baseline datasets were crucial for developing filtering protocols and confidence bounds used in later experiments.
6

Dyneema Testing Protocols. Subsequent testing focused on Dyneema HB26, with a standardized protocol developed to ensure consistency. Each test included:
• Pre-test inspection of sample flatness and alignment,
• Verification of strain gauge continuity and data acquisition triggering,
• Post-test inspection of bar faces for indentation or misalignment marks,
• Control of striker bar velocity using laser gates or preset drop heights.
The average impact velocity ranged from 20–30 m/s, depending on target strain and fixture
rigidity. Strain histories were calculated from incident and transmitted pulse data using standard
Kolsky bar reduction equations. Data were filtered using low-pass frequency methods validated
through numerical convergence studies.
Observed Material Behavior. Dyneema samples exhibited non-linear compressive behavior
under high-rate loading, with visible evidence of delamination, internal buckling, and fiber kinking
in post-mortem inspections. These failure modes were consistent across multiple layups and
correlated with energy dissipation patterns identified in full-scale impact testing. High-resolution
CT scans confirmed that axial strain accumulation produced characteristic voiding and interlayer
separation. The results also revealed the importance of layup orientation and sample thickness.
Thinner specimens with off-axis fiber orientations showed earlier onset of shear banding and
localized failure, while symmetric layups displayed more diffuse strain patterns. Repetition of
tests under identical conditions produced consistent peak stress values and deformation profiles,
validating the reliability of the fixture and instrumentation.
Supplemental Quasi-Static Testing. To establish a strain-rate dependence baseline, companion quasi-static compression tests were performed using an Instron testing machine. These
tests spanned strain rates of 0.001–0.1 s−1 and helped interpret high-rate data trends by anchoring stress–strain curve envelopes. Where possible, the same geometry and fixture constraints
were used to ensure comparability with dynamic testing data. In total, the experimental testing campaign produced a statistically meaningful dataset encompassing multiple strain rates,
specimen geometries, fiber layups, and loading configurations. These results supported later
modeling work and were essential for validating computational predictions of damage morphology, strain localization, and material softening.
4.1.4

CT Imaging and 3D Visualization

Micro-computed tomography (micro-CT) played a critical role in post-mortem analysis of Dyneema®
specimens tested under high strain rate loading. The primary objective of CT imaging was to
non-destructively examine internal failure modes, such as delamination, void formation, and fiber
disruption, and correlate them with both test conditions and simulation outputs. This imaging approach enabled evaluation of the Kolsky bar’s ability to replicate damage patterns observed in
full-scale ballistic tests.
CT Imaging Workflow. Dyneema samples were scanned after testing using a high-resolution
X-ray micro-CT system with voxel resolutions on the order of 5–10 microns. Prior to scanning,
samples were stabilized with minimal mechanical restraint to avoid deformation during mounting.
To reduce reconstruction artifacts, specimens were mounted vertically and scanned using 360°
7

rotational passes. Image stacks were reconstructed using standard back-projection algorithms
and exported as grayscale TIFF series for further segmentation. In cases where delamination or
porosity signatures were subtle, scan parameters were adjusted to increase material contrast,
and pre-scans were conducted to refine the region of interest (ROI) before committing to fullresolution acquisition.
Segmentation and Analysis. Image segmentation and post-processing were performed using
AVIZO software. Thresholding techniques were calibrated to distinguish between high-density
UHMWPE fibers, resin-rich layers, and low-density voids. Particular emphasis was placed on
identifying interlaminar cracks, through-thickness delamination, and residual cutting damage
from specimen preparation. In some cases, AVIZO’s machine learning segmentation tools were
trialed to improve boundary detection in fiber-reinforced regions. After segmentation, internal
features were visualized using 3D volume rendering and orthogonal slicing. Key observations
included:
• Delamination patterns along known fiber interfaces,
• Cut-induced artifacts aligned with laser or hot knife edges,
• Crack networks initiating from high-strain regions identified via simulation.
These internal failure features were used to validate damage predictions from embedded
finite element models and to explain anomalies in stress–strain data from specific tests.
Comparison with Ballistic Damage. CT results from Kolsky bar-tested specimens were compared to similar scans from Dyneema panels subjected to full-scale ballistic impact at LANL. The
damage morphologies were found to be qualitatively similar: both displayed internal splitting and
laminate separation at sub-millimeter scales. This correlation reinforced the utility of miniature
Kolsky testing as a surrogate method for exploring high-rate material failure under more controlled conditions.
3D and VR Visualization. To enhance interpretability and support collaboration with modeling
teams, segmented CT volumes were also imported into the Unity game engine for interactive
visualization. Custom scripts converted AVIZO outputs into OBJ surface meshes compatible
with Unity’s 3D rendering pipeline. This allowed investigators to explore damage topologies using
mouse or VR-based interfaces, including on Oculus Go headsets. The VR visualization workflow
enabled immersive inspection of complex features such as through-thickness crack propagation
and fiber bridging, which are otherwise difficult to interpret in traditional 2D slices. In total, the CT
imaging pipeline, from specimen preparation to interactive 3D visualization, provided an essential
feedback loop between experiment and simulation, bridging mesoscale material behavior with
macroscale failure interpretations.
4.1.5

Error Characterization and Statistical Testing

Ensuring repeatability and quantifying uncertainty were central to the development of a reliable
miniature Kolsky bar testing framework. Throughout the project, a rigorous statistical testing
methodology was adopted to identify potential sources of error, benchmark signal quality, and
construct confidence intervals for key experimental metrics. This approach enabled assessment
of test-to-test variability and informed decisions on fixture refinement, filtering strategies, and
data post-processing.
8

Baseline Characterization with No-Sample Tests. A comprehensive series of no-sample
tests (i.e., tests without specimens) was conducted to characterize system-level variability in
signal shape, amplitude, and noise level. These tests isolated contributions from striker impact
variability, bar misalignment, instrumentation sensitivity, and fixture compliance. Over 40 such
tests were performed across different bar materials, launch setups, and strain gauge placements. Strain gauge signals were processed using consistent filtering methods (e.g., low-pass
Butterworth filters with cutoffs between 100–300 kHz) to evaluate noise suppression strategies
and their effect on signal fidelity.
Error Sources and Mitigation Strategies. As the testing campaign matured, several dominant sources of experimental variability were identified and systematically addressed. These
included mechanical alignment issues, launch inconsistencies, and signal integrity problems. Table 1 summarizes the root causes and engineering interventions implemented to reduce them.
Collectively, these improvements led to more stable waveforms and significantly reduced standard deviations in measured strain.

4.1.6

Pulse Shaping and Data Filtering

Accurate interpretation of high strain rate experiments depends critically on the shape and clarity
of the stress wave signals transmitted through the Kolsky bar system. While this project primarily
focused on specimen development, system calibration, and data acquisition, foundational efforts
were also made to investigate pulse shaping and digital signal filtering techniques as avenues
for future refinement.

10

Preliminary Pulse Shaping Investigations. During the latter phase of Tom Hannah’s tenure
at Penn State, exploratory testing was conducted to evaluate the potential benefits of mechanical pulse shaping for the miniaturized Kolsky bar system. Concepts such as material-based
shapers (e.g., copper disks), striker geometry adjustments, and fixture compliance tuning were
considered as methods to improve stress wave separability and equilibrium attainment in soft
composite materials. However, these studies remained in the preliminary phase, and no formal
pulse shaping protocols were implemented during the experimental campaigns described in this
report. Future work will build upon these early investigations to systematically explore pulse
shaping strategies aimed at refining incident pulse characteristics and optimizing data quality for
soft materials like Dyneema® .
Digital Signal Filtering. While mechanical pulse shaping remains an area of future development, digital signal filtering was actively applied to enhance data clarity. Raw strain gauge signals
were processed using MATLAB-based routines to attenuate high-frequency noise introduced by
electronic circuitry and fixture vibration. Filtering parameters were carefully selected to preserve:
• Rise time and shape of incident and transmitted pulses,
• Peak amplitude fidelity,
• Separation between overlapping waveforms,
• Consistency across repeated tests.
Low-pass Butterworth filters with cutoff frequencies ranging from 100 kHz to 500 kHz were
utilized depending on bar material and gauge placement. Filter settings were refined iteratively
through spectral noise analysis and waveform comparisons to simulation outputs.
Signal Alignment and Time Zero Correction. For consistent calculation of strain and stress
histories, all experimental signals were aligned to a time-zero reference point established through
no-sample baseline tests. Phase shifts introduced by filtering were corrected using inverse filtering techniques and calibrated against expected pulse propagation timings.
Simulation Comparisons and Future Refinements. Filtered experimental waveforms were
compared to finite element simulations to assess signal fidelity and equilibrium behavior. These
comparisons informed the refinement of filtering parameters and highlighted areas where mechanical pulse shaping could further improve waveform quality. While this report documents the
groundwork for signal conditioning protocols, dedicated pulse shaping campaigns are planned
as part of future work to fully leverage the diagnostic capabilities of the miniaturized Kolsky bar
platform.
4.1.7

Bearing Design and Alignment Verification

Precise alignment and mechanical stability of the miniature Kolsky bar system were essential to
achieving repeatable, high-quality experimental results. Given the small bar diameter (3.16 mm)
and sensitivity of soft materials like Dyneema® to off-axis loading, particular attention was paid
to the design of bar support bearings and the verification of coaxial alignment between incident,
transmission, and striker bars. Initial bearing mounts were machined from aluminum and incorporated press-fit bushings to allow free sliding of the bars with minimal radial play. However, early
tests revealed subtle misalignment and bar wobble, which manifested as inconsistent signal rise
11

times and anomalous reflections even in no-sample tests. These issues prompted a redesign
of the bearing mounts to increase stiffness and improve the tolerance stack-up between the bar
outer diameter and the inner diameter of the bushings. The revised mounts used low-friction
linear bearings held in modular clamping blocks that allowed for easier replacement and fine
adjustment.
Bar alignment was verified using both mechanical and optical techniques. Feelers and
straight-edge tools were initially used to assess gross misalignment. For more sensitive verification, laser alignment tools were employed to ensure that the centerline of each bar section
remained within a specified envelope over the entire bar length. This was particularly important in the transition region near the specimen interface, where angular deviations as small
as 0.5 degrees could introduce non-uniform stress states during loading. Simulations using
Abaqus/Explicit were also conducted to quantify the mechanical consequences of misalignment.
These models showed that even small angular offsets at the bar-specimen interface could lead
to asymmetric wave transmission, non-uniform stress distributions in the specimen, and exaggerated reflected wave amplitudes. In particular, frictional interactions at the interface became
highly sensitive to bar tilt, leading to erroneous stress measurements and premature specimen
damage.
To mitigate these effects, the test fixture was modified to incorporate compliant alignment
guides that allowed micro-adjustments without introducing mechanical backlash. Specimens
were mounted using a low-pressure clamping mechanism that minimized lateral constraints while
preserving axial stability. Bar ends were polished and inspected after each test cycle to identify
concentric wear patterns or rotational scoring, both indicative of persistent misalignment. Over
time, these enhancements led to improved reproducibility of stress–strain curves and reduced
variability in waveform characteristics. Subsequent no-sample runs and repeated Dyneema tests
showed reduced signal noise and increased symmetry between incident and transmitted pulses,
validating the effectiveness of the alignment corrections. The iterative design and validation of
bearing supports and alignment protocols proved essential in achieving the mechanical precision required for miniature Kolsky bar testing. These refinements enabled more accurate interpretation of dynamic material response and improved consistency between experiment and
simulation.
4.1.8

Finite Element Model Integration

Throughout the project, finite element modeling played a central role in interpreting experimental results, refining test procedures, and establishing confidence in observed material behavior.
The integration of simulations with physical testing allowed for direct comparisons of wave propagation dynamics, stress distributions, and deformation patterns under high strain rate loading.
Finite element models were used not only to simulate specimen response but also to assess
system-level phenomena such as bar compliance, fixture friction, and misalignment sensitivity.
Initial modeling efforts focused on replicating no-sample Kolsky bar tests to establish baseline
wave characteristics. Using Abaqus/Explicit, three-dimensional bar geometries were constructed
with boundary conditions mirroring the physical test fixture. Simulations were configured to
match striker velocities observed in laser-gated tests, typically ranging from 20 to 30 meters
per second. These models captured the evolution of the incident, reflected, and transmitted
stress waves and were used to verify the timing and symmetry of experimental signals. Once
baseline validation was established, the focus shifted to simulating actual material specimens.
PMMA was used as the first test case due to its well-characterized rate-dependent mechanical
12

behavior and established material parameters. The numerical simulations reproduced the full
loading cycle of the miniature Kolsky bar setup, allowing for evaluation of stress wave equilibrium within the sample and the onset of plastic deformation. Comparison with experimental data
confirmed that equilibrium was typically reached within 3–6 wave reflections, consistent with
theoretical expectations for short samples with low L/D ratios.
More complex simulations involving Dyneema® specimens were developed using embedded
element modeling techniques. These models incorporated orthotropic material definitions and
embedded truss elements aligned with known fiber orientations, enabling more realistic capture of directional stiffness and failure progression. To support these models, bar-specimen
contact interfaces were carefully tuned, and multiple mesh densities were trialed to assess numerical convergence. In addition to stress–strain predictions, the models were used to track
strain localization, wave dispersion, and bar-end stress concentrations. One of the key benefits
of the integrated modeling approach was the ability to perform sensitivity analyses that would
be difficult or infeasible to conduct experimentally. For example, the effect of misalignment was
explored by intentionally offsetting bar axes in the model, which revealed substantial deviations
in stress uniformity and force transmission. Similarly, the effect of friction at the bar-specimen
interface was examined by varying surface roughness and contact pressure conditions, allowing
more informed design of specimen holders and alignment protocols. Simulations also served
as a design tool during iterative refinement of the experimental system. When certain waveform anomalies were observed in early tests, finite element models were used to test hypotheses about fixture stiffness, bar length ratios, and pulse shaping strategies. This feedback loop
enabled more efficient convergence on high-quality test configurations and improved the interpretability of experimental data. In later stages of the project, results from simulations were
compared directly with micro-CT imaging of post-mortem specimens. This qualitative and quantitative validation effort strengthened confidence in the ability of the models to reproduce not just
macroscopic stress–strain trends, but also internal damage mechanisms such as delamination
and fiber kinking. The tight integration of finite element modeling with experimental workflows
allowed the project to achieve a higher level of physical insight, reduced design iteration time,
and enhanced validation of observed material behaviors under extreme loading conditions.
4.1.9

Material Characterization and Layup Design

Comprehensive material characterization was essential for interpreting the mechanical response
of Dyneema® in both experimental and computational contexts. As a fiber-reinforced composite
with highly anisotropic properties, Dyneema presents unique challenges in understanding how
its laminate structure responds to high strain rate loading. This section summarizes efforts to
evaluate its constituent behavior, develop layup models, and establish quasi-static baselines to
support future Kolsky bar testing. At the outset, material datasheets and prior ballistic testing
literature were reviewed to establish initial assumptions regarding Dyneema’s through-thickness
stiffness, fiber yield strength, and failure mechanisms. However, due to proprietary formulations
and variability across production batches, empirical characterization was prioritized. Quasi-static
compression and tension tests were conducted using an Instron universal testing machine to
measure modulus, yield strength, and failure characteristics under low strain rate conditions.
These results provided critical data for simulation calibration and experimental planning to avoid
overdriving specimens during high strain rate tests.

13

Layup Design and Fiber Orientation. The modeling efforts focused on representing Dyneema®
laminates using idealized [0◦ /90◦ ] stacking sequences, which are commonly employed in commercial armor-grade composites. Embedded element finite element models were developed to
capture the alternating ply architecture and assess how fiber packing arrangements influence
macroscopic mechanical response. Simulations explored adjustments to fiber placement density, such as reducing inter-fiber spacing, to increase the modeled fiber volume fraction and
improve in-plane stiffness representation. These packing variations were used to evaluate the
scalability of the embedded element approach and its sensitivity to mesh resolution. While alternative layup configurations with off-axis fiber orientations were discussed conceptually, no models beyond the standard [0◦ /90◦ ] stacking were implemented during this phase of the project.
Computational studies demonstrated that increasing fiber volume fraction improved the representation of in-plane stiffness and could significantly influence failure patterns, such as delamination onset and anisotropic stress distribution. These findings, though limited to the [0◦ /90◦ ]
configuration, provided valuable insights for guiding future experimental fabrication and model
refinement strategies.
Surrogate Materials and Sensor Integration. PMMA was explored as a computational surrogate material due to its homogeneous properties and well-characterized mechanical response.
While PMMA was not used in physical experiments during this project, finite element models were developed to evaluate wave propagation behavior and validate simulation workflows.
PMMA remains a candidate for future experimental studies aimed at system calibration and
benchmarking. Preparatory work was also completed to support the eventual integration of
optical strain sensors into Dyneema laminates for full-field deformation tracking. Although these
sensors were not implemented during the reporting period, specimen preparation protocols were
designed to accommodate their inclusion in subsequent test campaigns without significantly altering specimen behavior. The material characterization and layup design efforts established a
strong computational foundation for future experimental work. The studies enabled controlled
variation of laminate structures in simulation, providing critical insights into failure mechanisms
and guiding the development of methodologies for high strain rate testing of fiber-reinforced
composites.
4.1.10

Supplementary Test Development

In addition to the primary Kolsky bar testing campaigns, several supplementary experimental efforts were undertaken to extend the platform’s capabilities, support future diagnostic integration,
and explore novel test modalities. These efforts were exploratory in nature but contributed valuable insights to fixture design, sensor implementation, and high-fidelity data acquisition strategies.
One significant area of focus was the development of a laser-based optical strain measurement system, spearheaded by Jeremy Croom during the latter phase of the project. Motivated
by the need for full-field, non-contact deformation tracking under high strain rates, this system
was designed to complement traditional strain gauges. Preliminary work involved optical bench
prototyping using a split-beam interferometer configuration with high-speed photodiodes and reflective markers. While these experiments were conducted independently of the Kolsky bar,
they demonstrated strong potential for integration into bar testing workflows. Calibration routines
were developed to convert photodiode signals into displacement histories, and synchronization

14

protocols were successfully tested using external trigger signals. These initial results provided a
promising foundation for future high-fidelity, optical strain diagnostics.

4.2

Computational Methods

High-rate impact modeling of fiber-reinforced composites such as Dyneema® requires accurate
numerical representations of material architecture, strain rate sensitivity, and dynamic equilibrium conditions. The computational work performed over the course of this project focused on
validating and extending embedded element finite element methods to replicate the behavior of
complex laminated composites under conditions comparable to those in miniature and full-scale
Kolsky bar experiments. This section outlines the modeling framework and simulation strategies
developed to support experimental results and to generalize findings for future predictive applications. Workflows were established in both commercial software (e.g., Abaqus/Explicit) and
open-source solvers (e.g., FLagSHyP), with customizations implemented for embedded fiber architecture, volume correction, and energy-based failure tracking. Special emphasis was placed
on evaluating mesh convergence, verifying time-step adequacy, and reproducing experimentally observed stress wave propagation behavior. In the following subsections, we describe
key developments in embedded element methodology, mesh and geometry generation, material
model calibration, Kolsky bar testbed simulation, and numerical validation. Where applicable,
simulation results are directly compared to experimental measurements, including stress-strain
response, timing of pulse reflections, and failure initiation sites. Together, these computational
efforts enabled the team to simulate full 3D specimens with resolved fiber architecture and to
explore the effects of geometry, defects, and material anisotropy on dynamic response.
4.2.1

Modeling Objectives and Embedded Element Framework

The primary computational goal of this project was to represent the mesostructure of fiberreinforced Dyneema® composites using an embedded element method (EEM). This framework
allowed discrete truss elements, representing high-modulus fibers, to be superimposed on a
host solid mesh that models the matrix material. Unlike homogenized continuum approaches,
this method preserves fiber orientation and architecture, enabling direct study of deformation,
stress transfer, and failure pathways through the composite. Two main motivations drove this
choice. First, the curved geometries and non-woven configurations typical of Dyneema panels
required a method that could flexibly align reinforcement directions without remeshing. Second,
EEM enabled coupling between micro-scale fiber mechanics and macro-scale loading conditions, bridging scales while maintaining computational feasibility.

15

Figure 4: Embedded fiber architectures explored in simulation. (a) Coarse fiber representation using a few
large-diameter truss elements. (b) Finer architecture with increased truss count and smaller diameters.
This study guided the tradeoff between accuracy and computational cost.

Figure 4 illustrates two contrasting approaches used to model Dyneema’s fiber content.
In subfigure (a), a coarse truss representation uses fewer, thicker fibers to approximate stiffness. In subfigure (b), a denser network of embedded trusses more closely mimics the in-plane
weave architecture of the actual laminate. This side-by-side comparison helped determine the
fidelity–cost balance and informed all subsequent mesh generation for embedded models. By
embedding these trusses without mesh conformity, the EEM strategy significantly reduced preprocessing time while allowing automated fiber generation within complex geometries. This innovation was critical to achieving scalable simulations capable of replicating high-rate experimental
tests.
4.2.2

Fiber Sensitivity and Unit Cell Simulations

To understand how fiber representation affects bulk mechanical response, a series of unit cell
simulations were conducted using simplified rectangular volumes with embedded truss elements
representing Dyneema fibers. These simulations varied the number and spatial layout of truss
elements while maintaining constant matrix dimensions, enabling isolation of geometric and volumetric effects. The primary objective was to determine an efficient fiber resolution that captures
anisotropic stiffness characteristics without incurring excessive computational cost.
Unit cells with progressively increasing fiber counts were subjected to uniaxial tension in the
Y-direction, while lateral faces were constrained to simulate periodic boundary conditions and
prevent rigid body motion. The core question addressed was: How many embedded fibers are
necessary to accurately represent the directional stiffness of the composite, while keeping simulation times tractable for large-scale explicit dynamics runs? Figure 5 illustrates the modeling
approach. Panel (a) shows unit cells with 0 fibers (pure matrix), 2 fibers, and 25 fibers em16

bedded within identical matrix volumes, highlighting the progressive scaling of fiber count within
the representative volume element (RVE). Panel (b) depicts the boundary conditions and loading configuration applied during the simulations. Tensile loads were applied to the top face of
the unit cell in the Y-direction, while lateral displacements were constrained to enforce uniform
deformation.
The simulation series revealed that mechanical stiffness increased with fiber count, eventually approaching an asymptotic limit indicative of convergence in directional stiffness prediction.
Models with low fiber counts significantly underpredicted axial stiffness due to insufficient fiber
representation, while models with very high fiber densities provided diminishing accuracy returns
relative to computational cost. An intermediate fiber density was therefore selected for subsequent embedded element simulations of full-scale Dyneema targets. This tradeoff maintained
fidelity in principal load-bearing directions while ensuring practical solve times. These unit cell
studies were critical for parameter selection in the embedded architecture, especially for dynamic impact simulations where strain localization and progressive failure mechanisms must be
accurately resolved.

Figure 5: Unit cell configurations and boundary conditions for fiber sensitivity simulations. (a) Embedded
fiber counts ranging from 0 to 25 fibers within a fixed matrix volume. (b) Boundary conditions applied
for uniaxial tension, with top-face loading in the Y-direction and lateral constraints to simulate periodic
boundaries.

4.2.3

Material Models and Mesh Design

The computational simulation of high strain rate deformation required careful attention to the constitutive models used and the discretization strategy employed in the finite element meshes. For
this project, Dyneema® and related composite materials were modeled using the Johnson-Cook
plasticity formulation, which provides a robust framework for describing strain rate-sensitive, thermally softening behavior in polymers and other ductile materials.
The Johnson-Cook model parameters were calibrated using a combination of published
quasi-static and high-rate data, supplemented with stress–strain predictions from embedded
fiber simulations. This approach enabled separation of matrix and fiber contributions to overall
stiffness and plastic response. As shown in Figure 6a, the simulation results closely track the
plasticity model and show reasonable agreement with literature-reported stress–strain behavior,
providing confidence in the model’s predictive capabilities.
To ensure numerical accuracy, we conducted a comprehensive mesh sensitivity study across
both the elastic and plastic deformation regimes. For the elastic regime, incident waveforms were

17

analyzed across a range of square mesh densities, from coarse (4×4) to fine (100×100). Figure 6b shows the resulting axial stress waveforms during impact. The overlay indicates minimal
deviation beyond a mesh size of 15×15, suggesting convergence had been reached for small
deformations. Figure 6c presents the peak axial stress recorded for each mesh configuration as
a function of mesh size. The stress plateau observed for meshes finer than 15×15 confirms that
spatial resolution was sufficient to capture elastic dynamics without artificial stress localization or
dispersion effects. In the plastic regime, however, stress waveform fidelity became increasingly
sensitive to mesh resolution, particularly under conditions involving fiber–matrix failure or crack
initiation. Figure 6d compares waveforms from simulations of dynamic impact with large plastic strains, showing growing divergence in waveform details at lower mesh densities. While the
general waveform shape remains consistent, higher-frequency content and peak stress magnitudes varied with mesh coarseness. These findings suggest that different mesh densities may
be needed depending on whether the simulation objective is to resolve stress wave propagation,
peak force prediction, or localized damage fields.

Figure 6: (a) Johnson-Cook plasticity model validation showing simulation results, fitted model, and experimental data from literature. (b) Elastic mesh convergence study: overlay of axial stress waveforms for
varying mesh sizes. (c) Maximum axial stress as a function of mesh density during elastic impact testing,
highlighting convergence behavior and mesh sensitivity. (d) Plastic deformation mesh study: waveform
comparison across mesh sizes, demonstrating limits of convergence in large deformation scenarios.

18

4.2.4

Kolsky Bar Simulation and Boundary Conditions

Simulating the PSU miniature Kolsky bar system required precise replication of both geometry
and dynamic boundary conditions to ensure accurate reproduction of experimental results. This
involved modeling the bar geometry, applying appropriate contact constraints between bars and
specimens, and generating incident stress waves consistent with striker impacts in physical tests.
The simulation domain was constructed to mirror the physical configuration of the miniature split
Hopkinson pressure bar, including aluminum input and output bars, the central specimen, and
fixture components that maintain axial alignment. Bar diameters and lengths were matched to
experimental setups, and the mesh density was refined near the interfaces to accurately capture
stress gradients and wave transmission. To replicate the dynamic loading conditions, stress
waves were generated using a velocity boundary condition at the end of the incident bar. The
velocity profile was calibrated based on experimental striker bar impacts, including both peak
magnitude and rise time, to ensure realistic wave shapes. These incident waves interacted
with the specimen and propagated through the output bar, allowing observation of reflected and
transmitted components.
Boundary conditions were applied with care to eliminate artificial constraints. The end of the
transmitted bar was fixed to represent the impact face against a rigid stop, while all lateral surfaces remained traction-free. Interface contacts between the specimen and bars were modeled
as frictionless or with minimal tangential resistance, depending on the test case. Although no image is included in this section, dynamic equilibrium was assessed by comparing axial stresses at
the two ends of the specimen during loading. These simulations demonstrated that stress levels
at both specimen-bar interfaces converged during the loading window, confirming proper implementation of wave propagation and boundary conditions. This foundation enabled subsequent
simulations involving more complex material models and defect-rich geometries, with confidence
that the mechanical input conditions replicated those of the physical bar system.
4.2.5

Validation with Experimental Data

To establish confidence in the computational model’s predictive capabilities, simulation results
were quantitatively compared against experimental measurements collected from the PSU miniaturized Kolsky bar system. This validation effort focused on two primary outputs: strain-time and
stress-time histories derived from strain gauge signals and post-processed through classical
Kolsky bar equations. The finite element simulations used two sets of material parameters: the
originally published Millan material model and a second, slightly adjusted set to better reflect
observed behavior. The incident and transmitted pulse data were reconstructed into equivalent stress and strain histories, which were then averaged across multiple experimental trials to
compute mean responses and associated 95% confidence intervals.
Figure 7 illustrates the degree of agreement between the numerical and experimental results.
For both strain and stress histories, the shape and timing of the primary pulse were well-captured
by the simulations. Notably, the adjusted parameter set reduced stiffness mismatch and brought
peak values into closer agreement with the mean experimental curves. However, the model still
slightly underpredicted strain magnitudes in the rising edge and showed higher frequency oscillations compared to the smoothed experimental signal. These deviations are likely attributable to
simplifications in the boundary condition representation and idealized assumptions about material homogeneity. Despite these limitations, the results demonstrate that the embedded element
fiber model, when coupled with calibrated matrix properties and fine mesh resolution, can re-

19

Figure 7: Comparison of finite element simulation results (dashed lines) to experimental mean signals
(solid lines) with shaded 95% confidence intervals for both (left) strain and (right) stress. Two FE models
are shown: one using Millan’s original material properties and one with adjusted parameters. Source:
Tom March 2024.

produce key dynamic behaviors observed in physical testing. This validation process not only
reinforces the credibility of the simulation methodology but also informs future model refinements,
particularly in material model tuning, damping treatment, and wave dispersion correction.

20

5

Results and Discussion

5.1

Introduction

This section presents the major experimental and computational findings of the project. It is
organized to first summarize physical test outcomes using the miniaturized Kolsky bar system,
followed by corresponding simulation results developed to reproduce and interpret the observed
behavior. The discussion emphasizes rate-dependent effects in Dyneema® -based composites
and the influence of fiber content, orientation, and manufacturing methods on failure morphology.
The experimental results draw from a multi-year series of high-strain-rate tests conducted on
aluminum and Dyneema laminates. Key insights include the successful validation of the miniaturized Kolsky bar system through classical aluminum benchmark tests, quantification of variability in bar signal response, and the identification of failure modes linked to fiber architecture
in Dyneema specimens. Advanced imaging methods, including scanning electron microscopy
(SEM) and X-ray computed tomography (CT), were employed to supplement stress–strain measurements and provide additional context for interpreting failure mechanisms.
On the computational side, embedded element models were developed to represent fiber–matrix
interaction under dynamic loading. These models were calibrated against experimental Dyneema
data and extended to simulate PMMA surrogates as a baseline for validating wave propagation
and equilibrium behavior. Computational studies focused on examining the role of internal fiber
architecture in strain localization, delamination onset, and damage evolution. Stress contour
plots and waveform overlays illustrate the correspondence between simulation and experiment,
highlighting areas of agreement as well as opportunities for further model refinement.
The combined approach enables a mechanistic understanding of high-rate behavior in protective composites and supports future design and modeling efforts at Los Alamos National
Laboratory. Experimental and simulation results are presented in paired subsections to facilitate
cross-comparison and interpretation.

5.2

Experimental Results

5.2.1

System Verification and Statistical Consistency

Before meaningful data could be collected from advanced materials such as Dyneema® , it was
critical to establish the repeatability and signal quality of the newly developed miniature Kolsky
bar system at Penn State. These verification efforts involved quantitative waveform analysis,
filtering optimization, and statistical benchmarking of no-sample tests to ensure that subsequent
material results reflected true dynamic behavior rather than experimental artifacts.
To this end, a series of 47 no-sample tests were conducted to characterize signal noise, mechanical alignment, and pulse shaping effectiveness, as detailed in Hannah et al. [1]. These tests
isolated system-level variability, including striker impact consistency, bar coaxiality, and instrumentation sensitivity. The system, featuring 3.16 mm diameter hardened steel bars supported
by lubricated brass bearings, was configured in a flipped orientation to minimize momentum trap
interference, as described by both Hannah et al. [1] and Grube et al. [6].
Figure 8 illustrates transmitted waveform variability from these tests. In part (a), variance
decomposition is shown for system, stress, area, and sample contributions. Part (b) presents
the standard deviation in sample and stress measurements over time, highlighting how initial
high-frequency variability diminishes as the test progresses into the plastic deformation regime.
21

Figure 8: Miniature Kolsky bar verification through waveform statistics, adapted from Hannah et al.
(2020) [1]. (a) Overlay of transmitted strain signal variances from repeated no-sample tests, decomposed
into system, area, stress, and sample contributions. (b) Comparison of sample and stress standard deviations over time, illustrating early noise and later consistency during plastic deformation.

In addition to variance-based diagnostics, the team also applied digital filtering techniques
to improve signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) and waveform fidelity. A low-pass Butterworth filter with
a cutoff frequency of approximately 250 kHz was found to effectively suppress high-frequency
noise while preserving the shape and timing of key waveform features such as pulse onset and
peak amplitude. Statistical metrics were computed to quantify test repeatability, as summarized
in Table 3. These metrics include peak transmitted stress variability, signal-to-noise ratio (SNR),
and onset jitter, all of which remained within acceptable bounds for high-rate testing of fiberreinforced composites. For example, peak transmitted stress values varied by less than 3.5%,
and onset times of compressive pulses jittered by under 0.5 µs, confirming system-level stability.
The same MATLAB tools used for waveform averaging and confidence interval analysis were
later applied to full-scale Dyneema and PMMA tests.
Table 3: Statistical metrics for evaluating test repeatability in the PSU miniaturized Kolsky bar system.

Metric

Purpose
