variability
Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR)

Evaluate load consistency across
runs
Quantify data quality pre- and postfiltering
Measure timing consistency of stress
wave arrival

Jitter in pulse onset time

Together, these findings confirmed that the PSU mini Kolsky bar system was not only mechanically robust but also capable of producing high-fidelity, repeatable data. This validation
provided a strong foundation for all subsequent material testing and simulation comparisons.
5.2.2

High-Rate Response of Dyneema Laminates

To evaluate the dynamic response of Dyneema® laminates under high strain rate loading, a
series of plate-on-plate impact experiments were performed using the PSU miniaturized Kolsky
22

bar system. These tests, as reported in Hannah et al. [2], investigated the influence of laminate
diameter and boundary conditions on internal damage morphology, with particular focus on fiber
deformation and failure angle evolution during ballistic penetration. Figure 9 presents CT scan
cross-sections of Dyneema specimens impacted under controlled high-speed loading. Three
plate diameters, 15.2 cm, 20.3 cm, and 25.4 cm, were tested to probe scale effects. In each case,
the projectile entered from the right side, and damage propagation was measured in degrees
relative to the axis of loading. Across all diameters, the failure consistently initiated through shear
plug formation followed by fiber snap-back, identified in the figure by red-circled zones. These
results reveal how increasing specimen diameter correlates with broader damage angles and
more distributed delamination. Fiber snap-back zones are especially prominent in the second
image of each test series, where internal recovery of tensioned fibers contributes to late-stage
failure. The quantified damage angles support ongoing modeling efforts aimed at reproducing
delamination progression and fiber pullout using embedded element techniques.

Figure 9: CT scans of Dyneema® laminates after high-speed impact for three plate diameters: 15.2 cm,
20.3 cm, and 25.4 cm. Each series shows two specimens: left: damage angle measurement; right: fiber
snap-back zones (circled). Projectile entered from the right in all cases. Adapted from Hannah et al.,
Experimental Mechanics, 2024 [2].

These imaging results emphasize the importance of geometric and architectural considerations in laminate design, particularly in contexts where fiber redirection and post-impact recovery
mechanisms dominate failure behavior. Future studies will link these CT-based damage profiles
to stress-strain data and simulation outcomes for improved model calibration.
5.2.3

Fiber Content and Structural Effects

To evaluate how internal fiber architecture influences the dynamic mechanical behavior of Dyneema®
composites, a series of high strain rate experiments were conducted using the PSU miniaturized
23

Kolsky bar. These tests focused on specimens with varying fiber volume fractions and lamination configurations to explore the relationship between structural design, strain rate response,
and failure characteristics. Figure 10 summarizes these results across four panels. Subfigure
(a) shows cylindrical composite specimens fabricated by B. Schuster’s group at the University of
Texas at El Paso (UTEP), where alternating 0/90 woven fiber layers were stacked and bonded to
form highly regular laminate geometries. The end-on and side views confirm uniform alignment
and consistent thickness across samples, enabling well-controlled loading and interpretation of
structural response. After testing, specimens exhibited distinct layer-wise failure features. Subfigure (b) highlights one such post-mortem micrograph, where step-like delamination and localized crushing occurred along the interfaces between alternating fiber layers. These interlaminar
failures suggest that compressive loads rapidly transitioned into shear-driven damage modes as
the wave propagated through the laminate. The combination of crushed zones and angled failure planes reflects classic snap-back and shear plug mechanisms often observed in UHMWPE
under dynamic loading.

Figure 10: Dynamic characterization of Dyneema® composite samples tested using the PSU mini Kolsky
bar. (a) Manufactured specimens from UTEP showing alternating woven fiber layers. (b) Micrograph of
failed sample highlighting layer-wise delamination and a crushed core. (c) Strain rate histories over time
for repeated tests, showing peak rates above 15,000 s−1 . (d) Dynamic stress–strain curves with ±2σ
bounds, indicating consistent behavior and abrupt failure onset.

The measured strain rate and stress histories from repeated tests are shown in subfigures (c)
and (d), respectively. Strain rate curves peaked near 15,000 s−1 within the first few microseconds
of loading, followed by a gradual decline as equilibrium was approached. The narrow confidence
intervals in the rising segment confirm the high repeatability of test conditions and pulse shaping protocols. Stress–strain curves displayed steep initial slopes corresponding to the high fiber
24

stiffness, followed by abrupt peaks and sharp post-peak drops—consistent with brittle interfacial
failure and limited post-yield deformation. The ±2σ bounds shaded in both plots emphasize the
statistical consistency of the results and validate the reliability of the Kolsky system for evaluating
fiber-reinforced composites. These results confirm that fiber volume fraction and layer architecture significantly influence both the dynamic stiffness and the failure behavior of Dyneema-based
composites. While increased fiber content enhances load-bearing capacity, it also amplifies the
tendency toward brittle interlaminar failure, particularly under extreme strain rates. These findings underscore the importance of structural tailoring in the design and simulation of protective
composite systems.

5.3

Failure Morphology and Imaging Results

Subsurface failure morphology of Dyneema® composites under high-rate impact was investigated using high-resolution X-ray computed tomography (CT). These imaging results provide
critical insight into the internal damage mechanisms that occur during ballistic loading, revealing
the sequence of events that follow initial projectile penetration. The figures and findings discussed here are adapted from Hannah et al. [2], published in Experimental Mechanics (2024) [2].
Figure 11 presents orthogonal cross-sectional CT images of a Dyneema laminate taken in the
X–Z plane. The projectile entered from the left and induced a well-defined shear plug failure near
the impact surface. Downstream of this region, additional damage modes such as fiber snapback and axial bundle deflection are evident. The localized deformation and fracture features
are annotated in the image, with clear fiber bundle separation, curved failure planes, and fibermatrix disbonding zones. These observations confirm that the failure initiates with compressive
shear and evolves into tensile-dominated fiber recoil. Measured dimensions indicate the axial
and transverse extent of damage, which are important for calibrating delamination and shear
models in simulation.

25

Figure 11: Orthogonal CT scan cross-sections of Dyneema® composite after impact, adapted from Hannah et al. [2]. Labeled features include shear plug failure near the impact site and fiber snap-back zones
further downstream. These images show the progression from compressive failure to tensile recovery in
woven UHMWPE laminates.

Complementing this view, Figure 12 shows a full-plate CT scan in the frontal plane, capturing
the complete damage cone geometry. The projectile impact creates a central plug, followed by
a radial delamination zone characterized by diverging fiber paths and matrix cracking. The measured damage angle, averaging 18.5°, provides quantitative insight into the spread of damage
through the thickness of the composite. A remaining thickness of 4.64 mm in the unpenetrated
region underscores the material’s energy absorption capacity. These results validate simulation
predictions of damage spread and provide evidence for multiscale failure transitions: from shear
plug to fiber snap-back to delamination.

26

Figure 12: Frontal CT scan of a 2.54 cm × 15.2 cm Dyneema® laminate after high-speed impact, adapted
from Hannah et al. [2]. The figure illustrates the full damage cone, including shear plug, snap-back zone,
and delamination front. The average cone angle is 18.5°, and 4.64 mm of unpenetrated material remains
ahead of the projectile.

These CT imaging results provide high-fidelity evidence of the failure sequence in UHMWPE
composites under dynamic loading. The coupling of fiber-level and laminate-scale damage features, observable only through CT, highlights the critical role of subsurface mechanisms in overall
composite performance. These observations form a foundation for validating embedded failure
criteria and informing the development of high-fidelity finite element models.

27

5.4

Computational Results

5.4.1

Verification and Statistical Consistency of Kolsky Bar Simulations

To ensure the fidelity of finite element simulations used to replicate high-rate testing conditions,
we performed a verification study focusing on waveform consistency, modeling imperfections,
and mesh sensitivity. This effort is adapted from recent work by Hannah et al. [3], published
in the Journal of Verification, Validation and Uncertainty Quantification, and addresses the differences in response between axisymmetric and half-symmetry models of Kolsky bar systems
across two scales. Figure 13 presents results from this verification campaign. Subfigure (a)
shows the mesh configuration used for the small-scale 12.7 mm Kolsky bar simulations, where
the sample and incident/transmission bars are modeled with separate domains and mesh densities. The incident and transmission bars are extended virtually beyond the sample region, consistent with physical test setups, allowing for clean capture of wave propagation and reflections.
Subfigure (b) compares stress waveforms from axisymmetric and half-symmetry simulations for
both small and large systems. Reflected and transmitted wave signals are plotted to highlight
sensitivity to geometric modeling choices. The reflected wave in the smaller system shows clear
deviation between the two modeling approaches, particularly in the peak magnitude and timing, emphasizing how real-world imperfections such as bushing misalignment or bar end face
tilt may disproportionately affect miniature bar systems. In contrast, the large-scale system exhibits greater consistency across model types due to its larger mass and inherent averaging of
imperfections.

Figure 13: Verification of Kolsky bar finite element models, adapted from Hannah et al. [3]. (a) Mesh
layout for the small-scale (12.7 mm) Kolsky bar simulation showing sample and bar configuration. (b)
Comparison of reflected (top) and transmitted (bottom) stress waveforms for small (left) and large (right)
bars, highlighting differences between axisymmetric and half-symmetry models.

These results demonstrate that high-fidelity modeling of Kolsky bars requires careful attention
to symmetry assumptions, especially in small systems where imperfections have a more pronounced impact. The half-symmetry model captures features such as bar eccentricity, off-axis
loading, and imperfect boundary conditions, making it more representative of actual experimental behavior. The reflected wave in particular serves as a diagnostic for mechanical alignment
and contact fidelity in the simulation. This verification effort underscores the importance of mesh
quality, boundary realism, and numerical filtering in interpreting miniature Kolsky bar results. The
28

enhanced sensitivity of the smaller system to alignment errors supports the need for tight mechanical tolerances in experiments and validates the use of high-resolution models in predictive
simulation campaigns.
5.4.2

Fiber Representation and Dynamic Response

To capture the dynamic behavior of fiber-reinforced composites under high-rate loading, we developed embedded element finite element models that explicitly represent fiber content and orientation. This modeling framework enables the study of stiffness contributions from aligned
fibers, strain distribution across fiber–matrix boundaries, and sensitivity to volume fraction variation. Embedded truss elements were used to simulate fibers, while the surrounding matrix was
treated as a nonlinear elastic-plastic continuum. The dynamic response was simulated under
conditions mimicking a Kolsky bar test, with compressive loading applied through incident and
transmission bars. Figure 14 summarizes two key results adapted from Martin et al. [4]. Subfigure (a) illustrates three distinct truss packing schemes that were used to vary fiber volume fractThese configurations include original spacing, reduced spacing, and a densely packed [0◦ /90◦ ]
layup, allowing controlled exploration of fiber content while holding matrix properties constant.
The geometric fidelity of these embedded element representations reflects realistic tow architecture in Dyneema® laminates. Subfigure (b) compares the simulated compressive stress–strain
responses for embedded element models with different fiber densities to experimental data reported by Attwood et al. (2014). The simulation curves for 12 and 1000 embedded fibers per
truss closely track the experimental curve, particularly in the linear regime, demonstrating that
the embedded element formulation accurately reproduces composite stiffness. Importantly, the
figure shows that once a critical fiber content is reached, further refinement of fiber discretization
does not significantly affect macroscopic response, suggesting that a coarse representation may
be sufficient for large-scale simulations when fiber volume fraction is known.
These simulations confirm that fiber volume fraction directly controls effective composite stiffness under dynamic loading, while stress–strain behavior remains robust to fine-scale discretization when fibers are well-aligned. The ability to match experimental behavior with simplified fiber
models underscores the utility of the embedded element approach for predictive simulations.
This methodology provides a mechanistic link between fiber architecture and bulk composite
performance, enabling future design optimization studies for impact-resistant armor systems.
5.4.3

Damage Evolution and Failure Prediction

To evaluate internal failure processes in Dyneema® composites under high strain-rate loading,
embedded finite element simulations were performed incorporating both matrix plasticity and
fiber-level failure criteria. The models utilized strain-based deletion of embedded truss or beam
elements to represent fiber rupture, combined with continuum plasticity in the matrix phase to
simulate progressive damage accumulation and delamination. Figure 15 presents key simulation results adapted from Martin et al. [5]. Subfigure (a) compares two formulations: embedded
truss elements and embedded beam elements. While both capture overall damage morphology,
beam elements exhibit earlier onset of localized failure and reduced matrix delamination, albeit
at the cost of increased computational demand. Both models predict failure initiating near the
impact surface and progressing via axial cracking and fiber pull-out. In subfigure (b), simulation
results are overlaid with experimental CT scans, showing excellent agreement in the localization
of key failure mechanisms including shear plug formation, fiber snap-back, and interlaminar de29

Figure 14: Embedded element simulation of fiber-reinforced composites, adapted from Martin et al. [4].
(a) Truss packing configurations used to vary fiber volume fraction from 52.9% to 92.0%, simulating different manufacturing architectures. (b) Compressive stress–strain curves comparing embedded element
simulations to experimental data, showing good agreement and insensitivity to truss refinement at fixed
volume fraction.

lamination. Predicted damage zones correspond well with annotated experimental observations,
reinforcing the model’s ability to reproduce observed physical phenomena.
Subfigure (c) illustrates stress contour plots from embedded element simulations across varying fiber architectures. The left column highlights axial fiber tension and compression, while the
right column captures interlaminar shear concentrations. Increased fiber packing density elevates axial stiffness and concentrates failure in the matrix shear regions. Meanwhile, off-axis
layups promote more diffuse failure fields, consistent with experimental trends in strain distribution and damage spreading. These results demonstrate that the embedded element simulation
framework not only replicates macroscopic mechanical behavior but also accurately resolves
meso-scale failure evolution within the laminate. By capturing phenomena such as delamination, shear localization, and fiber rupture, the model provides a validated predictive tool for future
composite armor design and optimization.
5.4.4

Simulation–Experiment Comparison for Dyneema

The predictive performance of the embedded finite element framework was evaluated by comparing simulated impact responses to experimental deformation patterns in Dyneema® laminates.
These comparisons focus on both macroscopic features, such as deformation cones, and internal stress distributions, offering insight into how fiber orientation and layup architecture govern
dynamic failure. Figure 16 presents results adapted from Martin et al. [5]. Subfigures (a) and
(b) show experimentally observed front and back face images of impacted Dyneema targets.
The deformation cone structures, formed by the in-plane stretching and alignment of the principal fibers, are clearly visible and exhibit anisotropic propagation consistent with [0◦ /90◦ ] layups.
These mirrored patterns provide spatial evidence of tension along the fiber axes and shear deformation at orthogonal interfaces.
Subfigures (c) and (d) show the corresponding simulated von Mises stress fields generated
30

Figure 15: Simulation of Dyneema® composite damage progression under high-rate loading, adapted
from Martin et al. [5]. (a) Comparison of truss vs. beam element damage evolution in embedded models;
(b) overlay of predicted damage fields with experimental CT scans showing shear plug and delamination;
(c) stress contours showing axial tension/compression (left) and interlaminar shear (right) for varying fiber
architectures.

using the embedded element finite element method. The simulation reproduces the key morphological features of the deformation cone, including the X-shaped stress propagation along fiber
directions and the central plug-like damage zone. The front face (c) captures radial tension and
matrix constraint, while the rear face (d) exhibits shear localization and bending-driven delamination, both of which are consistent with experimental imaging. The spatial alignment between
modeled stress fields and observed deformation modes provides strong validation of the embedded modeling framework. These simulations not only replicate the directional nature of failure in
fiber-reinforced laminates but also capture the strain localization and progressive damage that
control impact resistance. Together, the agreement across experimental and computational results supports the use of this approach for predictive simulation and virtual design of advanced
composite systems.
5.4.5

Simulation of PMMA for Baseline Validation

Polymethyl methacrylate (PMMA) was selected as a reference material to validate the miniature Kolsky bar simulation framework due to its well-documented mechanical properties, ease
31

Figure 16: Experimental and simulated deformation fields in Dyneema® laminates under impact, adapted
from Martin et al. [5]. (a,b) Front and back face images of impacted specimens showing deformation
cones; (c,d) corresponding finite element simulations showing von Mises stress patterns and anisotropic
failure along fiber directions.

of machining, and optical transparency. These characteristics make PMMA an ideal baseline
for evaluating whether the simulation pipeline can accurately reproduce dynamic stress wave
propagation, equilibrium conditions, and material deformation under high strain rate loading.

32

Figure 17: Simulated stress–strain curve for PMMA under high strain rate loading using the PSU mini
Kolsky bar configuration, adapted from ASME IMECE 2024 proceedings. Dynamic equilibrium is achieved
after three (green) and six (red) wave cycles.

Finite element simulations were conducted in Abaqus/Explicit using an axisymmetric representation of the PSU miniature Kolsky bar system with 3.16 mm diameter bars. The PMMA sample was modeled using a Johnson–Cook plasticity formulation calibrated from Blumenthal’s constitutive data on rate-dependent PMMA behavior. Mesh convergence studies were performed to
ensure numerical stability, and virtual strain gauge locations were defined to extract axial stress
and strain histories from the specimen center and bar interfaces. The stress–strain trajectory in
Figure 17 exhibits characteristic wave cycles following initial elastic loading, with gradual hardening and clear yield behavior. Dynamic equilibrium is observed prior to peak stress, supporting
the reliability of the extracted mechanical response.

33

Figure 18: Stress–strain response and waveforms from PMMA simulation, adapted from Mach 2025.
Simulation confirms dynamic equilibrium and matches expected strain levels for uniaxial high-rate loading.

Figure 18 provides a comprehensive overview of simulation outcomes from the Mach 2025
campaign. The stress–strain curve reveals a maximum strain of 16.8% and demonstrates that
equilibrium is achieved at approximately 16 µs, in agreement with theoretical estimates. Additional waveform analysis confirms symmetric propagation and minimal reflections, reinforcing
that the simulation satisfies the key conditions for valid Kolsky bar operation. These findings
validate the accuracy of the simulation toolchain for benchmark materials. The agreement in
stress–strain behavior, wave timing, and equilibrium metrics demonstrates that the model reliably captures both the mechanical response and the physical assumptions underpinning Kolsky
bar mechanics. This PMMA reference case provides critical confidence in extending the same
modeling strategy to more complex, fiber-reinforced composites in subsequent simulations.

34

6

Conclusions

This final report documents the successful completion of a multi-year research effort aimed at
enabling high-fidelity, high strain rate characterization and simulation of fiber-reinforced polymer composites, with a particular focus on Dyneema® . Through the development of a custom
miniaturized Kolsky bar platform and advanced embedded element modeling tools, the project
achieved all major technical objectives under Subcontract Nos. 424285 and 587178.
Key conclusions are as follows:
1. A functional 3.16 mm mini Kolsky bar system was developed and validated. The system
supports dynamic testing of soft materials at strain rates exceeding 103 s−1 , with consistent
attainment of dynamic equilibrium and reproducible signal fidelity.
2. Dyneema HB26 specimens were successfully tested using this platform. The resulting
high-rate stress–strain curves showed expected anisotropic responses, and matched failure mechanisms observed in full-scale impact tests.
3. High-resolution CT imaging provided critical insight into internal damage morphology, including delamination, voiding, and fiber rupture. These scans were essential for comparing
experimental outcomes to simulation predictions.
4. An embedded element finite element modeling framework was developed and validated for
UHMWPE composites. These models accurately captured fiber-dominated anisotropy and
enabled predictive simulations of localized damage and strain propagation.
5. Modeling and experimental results were strongly aligned. Validated PMMA and Dyneema
simulations reproduced wave propagation dynamics, equilibrium states, and damage zones
identified via CT.
6. Several components of this effort—including the embedded modeling methodology and the
Kolsky bar platform—are now positioned for broader dissemination, training, and internal
use at LANL and collaborating institutions.
Collectively, this work provides a robust test-and-modeling methodology that addresses critical gaps in the dynamic characterization of penetration-resistant composites. It also offers validated tools and platforms that can support future LANL missions in armor material development, modeling and simulation, and probabilistic design verification. Notably, this collaborative
research was featured as the cover article of Experimental Mechanics (see Figure 19), underscoring its impact within the high-strain-rate mechanics community.

35

Figure 19: Cover art from Experimental Mechanics, Volume 64, Issue 6, July 2024. This issue featured
work from the LANL–Penn State collaboration on UHMWPE impact testing.

36

7

Recommendations

Based on the findings and validated outcomes of this work, the following recommendations are
offered to Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL) for continued development, integration, and
expansion of high strain rate characterization and simulation capabilities for composite armor
systems.

7.1

Deployment of Mini Kolsky Bar Platform

• Adopt the mini Kolsky bar system for in-house testing of UHMWPE and soft polymers. Its small footprint and proven reliability make it well suited for LANL materials labs
or satellite facilities.
• Implement standard operating procedures (SOPs) for test setup, pulse shaping, filtering, and data post-processing, based on protocols established in this project.
• Train additional personnel to operate the platform using a subset of known benchmark
materials such as PMMA, which allow for system health checks and calibration before
testing advanced composites.

7.2

Advanced Diagnostics and Strain Measurement

• Complete and integrate the laser-based full-field strain measurement system developed during the final project phase. When combined with the mini Kolsky bar, this system
would enable local strain tracking and validation of mesoscale deformation models.
• Incorporate digital image correlation (DIC) or stereo high-speed imaging to complement the CT-based damage assessments and capture in situ surface deformation during
dynamic loading.
• Evaluate the potential for IR thermography during Kolsky bar tests to quantify adiabatic
heating and relate thermal fields to deformation localization in fiber-reinforced composites.

7.3

Modeling and Simulation Extensions

• Deploy the embedded element modeling workflows in LANL-supported simulation
environments. The methodology is compatible with both Abaqus/Explicit and FLagSHyP,
and its validation with Dyneema offers a strong use case.
• Extend embedded element models to handle probabilistic damage evolution and interfacial failure, especially in curved geometries or composite joints. These features are
relevant for armor panel assemblies.
• Develop surrogate models using embedded element outputs to enable real-time prediction of damage zones or strain fields during high-rate events.

7.4

CT-Based Validation and Feedback Loops

• Expand the use of CT imaging pre- and post-test to create paired simulation-validation
datasets. These can serve as a basis for data-driven model calibration.

37

• Standardize CT scanning protocols and segmentation workflows for internal fiber
alignment, delamination zones, and crack evolution in polymer composites.
• Explore AI/ML-assisted segmentation techniques to speed up damage quantification
and classification of failure modes across multiple Dyneema samples.

7.5

Broader Scientific and Programmatic Integration

• Integrate this work into broader LANL composite armor programs where repeatable
small-scale testing and embedded modeling can reduce reliance on full-scale plate testing.
• Support cross-site knowledge transfer and open documentation so that the test methods, embedded models, and CT workflows can be adopted across LANL divisions or
shared with partners.
• Consider follow-on studies focused on temperature, humidity, and fatigue effects in
Dyneema and related laminates, especially under high-rate cyclic or multi-hit conditions.
These recommendations build on the foundation of this project to enhance LANL’s capabilities
in both experimental and computational analysis of dynamic composite material behavior.

VERIFICATION AND VALIDATION OF A NOVEL MINITURIZED KOLSKY BAR USING
PUBLISHED POLYMETHYL METHACRYLATE (PMMA) DATA
Ryan N. Grube
The Pennsylvania State
University
University Park, PA, 16802

Thomas W. Hannah
Los Alamos National
Laboratory
Los Alamos, NM, 87545

Valerie A. Martin
Los Alamos National
Laboratory
Los Alamos, NM, 87545

Stephen Ellis
Los Alamos National
Laboratory
Los Alamos, NM, 87545

Reuben H. Kraft
The Pennsylvania State
University
University Park, PA, 16802

ABSTRACT
The Kolsky bar experiment is used to determine material
properties of various materials at high strain rates. This paper
aims to use published data of Kolsky bar experiment stress
versus strain results to establish a benchmarking methodology to
validate and verify a novel miniaturized Kolsky bar. Creations of
a Johnson-Cook plasticity and damage model are implemented
into an Abaqus Finite Element simulation of a novel miniaturized
Kolsky bar to simulate the mechanical material properties of
polymethyl methacrylate (PMMA) at high strain rates. The
Finite Element simulation is proved to produce viable results
through establishing proper wave propagation throughout the
Kolsky bar system and replication of the Johnson-Cook plasticity
model results. A comparison of experimental and simulation data
will result in the declaration of the validity of the novel
miniaturized Kolsky bar for PMMA and its material class. Due
to the sensitivity of the experimental system, data analysis for
statistical evidence is needed to prove that the experimental data
matches the Johnson-Cook model prediction after a large
population of properly setup experiments are performed. Future
research is determined to be beneficial for establishing material
properties of more elastic and ductile materials at high strain
rates for biomedical purposes.
Keywords: Finite Element, Mechanical Material Properties,
Wave Propagation, Data Analysis

1. INTRODUCTION
Split Hopkinson Pressure bar or Kolsky bar
experiments are used to measure material properties at high
strain rates1-8. Various materials such as alloys, polymers, and
ceramics have all been explored using Kolsky bar experiments112
. Of these materials, Polymethyl Methacrylate (PMMA) serves
as a good verification material for establishing confidence for
Kolsky bar experiments and establishing modeling frameworks
for more complex polmer-like materials. It is common for
Kolsky bar systems to have bar diameters of about 10-25mm.
These Kolsky bar systems can test samples in the strain rate
range of 102s-1 to 103s-1. Smaller bars of a diameter of about 3mm
can test in a range of 104s-1 to 105s-1 (1-12). At higher strain rates,
it is common to see greater amounts of plasticity in the sample.
Because the material properties of PMMA are strain rate and
temperature dependent, it can be considered for a good material
choice as a benchmark sample for Kolsky bar experiments. In
addition to the PMMA’s material properties, the transparent
qualitative property of PMMA allows for the visualization of
damage propagation and magnitude. In this paper, in order to
replicate and instill confidence in Kolsky bar experiments, the
Johnson-Cook (JC) plasticity and damage models are
incorporated into a Finite Element (FE) analysis using the
analysis package Abaqus®. Conveniently, the JC model
incorporates the effects of strain rate and temperature into
determining the flow stress and material behavior. The aim of
this research is to determine a verification and validation process
for all Kolsky bar systems to ensure useful benchmarking at high

2. METHODS
2.1 Kolsky Bar System
The Kolsky bar system is comprised of four main
components, the incident, transmission, and striker bars and the
sample. Each of the bars are of the same diameter and are made
from the same material, usually maraging steel or an aluminum
alloy1-12. A sample is tested by placing the desired testing
material between the incident and transmission bars. By
impacting the incident bar with the striker bar, which is launched
by the release of a high pressure source, the sample is
compressed between the incident and transmission bar. The
Kolsky bar system that we are using for our experimental
research is Penn State’s in house miniature Kolsky bar system
with each bar having a diameter of 3.16mm. Additionally, the
incident and transmission bars are 812.8mm long and the striker
bar is 99.16mm long. The bars are held in place by brass bearings
that are lubricated with a silicone-based lubricant to provide
minimal frictional force on the bars. With minimal friction, the
strain gauges attached to the incident and transmission bars can
accurately read the wave propagation sent through the system.
The strain gauges used have a resistance of 350 Ohms, gauge
length of 1mm and gauge width of 1.8mm, allowing accurate
results and minimum strain gauge bending. The electrical
impulse sent from the strain gauges is recorded by a Dewetron
TRIOnet power analyzer to retrieve a strain vs time graph of the
experiment. For more details on the electrical measurement
system, see reference Hannah et al. (6). Figure 1 from Hannah
et al. (6) depicts a properly setup Kolsky bar system and the
locations of the applied strain gauges and brass bearings. Using
the data found through our Kolsky bar experiments and
comparing them to our simulations, the miniaturized Kolsky bar
system can be proved to export accurate results. Statistical
evidence and methodology for ensuring reproducibility for this
process is detailed in reference Hannah et al. (6)

temperature, and 𝑇𝑇𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟 is the reference temperature13-17. This
study does not consider thermal softening because the published
research and our experiments are conducted at room
temperature. The published data that we are using for our
research is from W. R. Blumenthal et al. (4), who produced
compression stress-strain measurements of PMMA samples in a
Kolsky bar system at varied strain rates at room temperature. A
reference strain rate of 1s-1 is commonly used, but the published
research did not provide data for this rate. Instead, a strain rate
of 5s-1 from the published data is used because more data lies in
the plastic region, allowing a better fit for greater strain rates than
data from a strain rate of 0.1 s-1 while remaining close to the
commonly used reference strain rate. With the reference strain
rate and equations for other variables in the JC model
established, values for the material constants in the JC model can
be found. When the experimental strain rate is equal to the
reference strain rate and the deformation temperature is equal to
the reference temperature in Equations 2 and 3 respectively, the
equation for true stress becomes13-17:
𝜎𝜎 = 𝐴𝐴 + 𝐵𝐵𝜀𝜀 𝑛𝑛 .

determined through a linear fit. Rearranging Equation 4 and
taking the natural logarithm results in Equation 5 as shown
below13-17:

2.2 Johnson-Cook Plasticity Model
The material relationship between stress and strain
under high strain rates, elevated temperatures, and extreme
deformations can be observed through the Johnson-Cook
model13-17. The Johnson-Cook model is comprised of three
sections with each contributing a different aspect of material
deformation. The stress model is expressed as shown13-17:

Values for σ and ε at the reference strain rate, as well as A, the
yield stress, are substituted into Equation 5 to plot a linear
relationship. The first-order regression plot is presented in Figure
2 with an R2 value of 95.69%, denoting that there is a high
correlation with the experimental data points and the linear
regression.

strain rates. By implementing a JC model for PMMA into an FE
simulation, truly representative results can be generated for
comparison with experimental data.

The loading condition is applied to the bottom of the sample
to initiate an axial force. The first boundary condition is applied
to the left side of the sample, the center axis, to ensure there is
only axial displacement about the center. The second boundary
condition is set to the top surface of the sample and restricts axial
movement and rotation to simulate a compressive force. The
sample is shown with all of the loading and boundary conditions
in Figure 6. The results of the job are shown in Figure 7 and when
compared to the JC model results, they are approximately exact.
The results of the simulation give confidence that the simulation
is set up correctly and the plasticity model is correct due to
reproduced results.

2.4 Finite Element Hierarchical Validation Process
To see if simulation results will reproduce results seen from
the JC plasticity model, a static general simulation of a single
element axis-symmetric model of the proposed PMMA sample
was used. An instantaneous loading condition and boundary
conditions are used to simulate a sample being compressed to its
ultimate compression strength. The elastic material model and
the loading and boundary conditions used for the PMMA sample
can be found in Table 2 and Table 3 respectively.

methodology for the JC plasticity model applies to the damage
model.

Figure 7: Comparison of the JC plasticity model and Abaqus FE
simulation to the published data. The JC model produces slightly
greater stress values compared to the Abaqus FE simulation.

2.5 Sample Mesh Convergence Study
Before implementing the sample’s plasticity material model
and simulating the Kolsky bar experiment in an explicit dynamic
FE simulation, a mesh convergence study is completed of an
elastic simulation of the Kolsky bar model. The FE axissymmetric Kolsky bar modeling structure with a diameter of
3.16mm used in Hannah et al. (7) will be used to conduct the
mesh convergence study and additional simulations. The bars are
made from 7075 aluminum and when compared to steel, provide
a better system sensitivity for testing softer materials due to the
better impedance match [16]. The greater system sensitivity will
provide more accurate data once filtered for determining the
plasticity of PMMA samples. The material model for the bars
and the loading and boundary conditions used to complete the
mesh convergence study are found in Table 4 and Table 5
respectively. The elastic material model for the sample from
Table 2 will be reused in the mesh convergence study. Due to the
difference in material properties for the bar and sample in this
study compared to Hannah et al. (7) a mesh convergence study
is conducted to ensure accurate simulation results for the new
materials.

various mesh densities. The finer the mesh density gets, the more
accurate and less sporadic the wave.

Copyright © 2024 by The United States Government

Downloaded from http://asmedigitalcollection.asme.org/IMECE/proceedings-pdf/IMECE2024/88681/V010T12A011/7428008/v010t12a011-imece2024-145918.pdf by The Pennsylvania State University user on 26 June 2025

The incident and transmission bars have a mesh density
of 8 radial elements that are 0.334mm in length, 8x0.334mm.
The striker bar has a mesh density of 4x1mm to transfer the wave
through the incident bar with accuracy. For the sample, the mesh
density is increased in an iterative process to determine when an
element converges to an axial compressive stress value
approximately equal to the previous mesh density’s compressive
stress. The sample mesh must possess a finer mesh than the mesh
used for the incident and transmission bars because of the higher
elasticity and lesser strength of PMMA. Due to the material
properties of PMMA, a finer mesh will capture the plastic
deformation properly by identifying the deformation on smaller
increments. The Figures 8 and 9 represent the impact wave
transmitted through the sample at different mesh densities and
the maximum axial compressive stress seen for an element in an
approximate area for different mesh densities respectively. The
data present in the figures determines that at a mesh density of
50 elements long and 50 elements wide provides a converged
solution for the elastic region of the material model for the
sample.

and the maximum resultant axial stress seen at the strain gauge
location of a physical experiment. The data shows that having 16
elements radially presents a converged mesh density for the
incident bar’s radius because of a similar waveform and
maximum axial stress seen from the denser 20 element radial
mesh. Since the length of the elements is approximately .334mm
with the strain gauge’s gauge length equaling approximately
1mm, three elements encompassing the strain gauge area on the
outer radius of the axis-symmetric simulation are averaged for
accurate data. The mesh density was changed by one aspect at a
time and only once a converged value was found, was the
incident bar mesh density changed for further mesh density
convergences.

resultant axial stress at the strain gauge location. Like the radial
element convergence study, an average was taken on the outer
radius elements encompassing the strain gauge length of 1mm.

mesh density for the incident and transmission bar is required to
produce accurate results to estimate the stress and strain seen in
the sample from the strain gauge positions. The same material
models for the bars and sample as well as the determined mesh
density for the sample are used to complete the bar’s mesh
convergence study. Figures 12 and 13 represent the impact wave

2.8 Dynamic Equilibrium of the Sample
For the data to be valid, the sample must be in
dynamic equilibrium to represent the strain seen in the sample
as instantaneous. Identification of dynamic equilibrium is
procured by determining when the sample stress transmission
reaches three to six wave cycles. To computationally determine
if the sample is in dynamic equilibrium, the elastic wave speed
of the sample, 𝐶𝐶𝑠𝑠 , is found to determine the sample’s wave
cycle, or time for an elastic wave to move from one free surface
to the other and back. The equation:

The same process for the incident bar was performed
for the transmission bar. Results from the mesh convergence
study justifies the transmission bar to have the same mesh
density as the incident bar. A mesh convergence study can be
performed on the striker bar, but it was decided the work that
would be completed would not be beneficial enough for the
time spent on the process. The striker bar’s job is to relay a
force into the incident bar, and does not require a dense mesh to
do so accurately. There are also no calculations that are needed
from any location on the striker bar. Due to these reasons, the
striker bar will be kept at a mesh density of 4 elements radially
with a length of approximately 1mm.

found in Table 2. The resultant elastic wave speed in the sample

2.7 Sample Aspect Ratio Convergence Study
The length to diameter aspect ratio of the sample is
necessary to determine accurate results from a Kolsky Bar
experiment. An aspect ratio between 0.6 and 1 is determined to
be a good range to conduct experiments to reduce the inertial
and frictional forces produced by the sample to bar area
mismatch [8]. Using a proper aspect ratio for Kolsky bar
experiments can be visualized through the tensile reflection
wave in the incident bar not equating to zero until a
compressive reflection wave reaches the strain gauge position.
Any sample aspect ratio larger than 1 is seen as using excess

as seen in [10, eq. (1.1)]. Equation (14) finds that one wave

material and does not allow the Kolsky Bar to achieve its

sample aspect ratios is demonstrated in the reflection wave
extrema and wave magnitude. Experiments in this paper
continue with a sample aspect ratio of 0.8 as it was determined
to have a continuous tensile reflection wave until the true
compressive region. This convergence study is necessary as the
results found from the reflection wave are used to estimate the
strain seen in the sample. The final size of the sample has a
diameter of 2.53mm and a height of 2.025mm. Compared to
Penn State’s Kolsky bar diameter of 3.16mm, it is 80% smaller.
This should allow for 30% true strain of the sample until the
bar’s diameter is exceeded [8].

absolute value of the stress wave in the transmission bar and the
tensile reflection wave in the incident bar. By comparing the
incident wave transmission to the combined wave transmission,
it can be concluded that the stress acting on the sample is
consistent throughout the experiment. This can be seen in
Figure 17. Using equation (14) and the associated 3t to 6t
durations, it can be it determined that the sample reaches
dynamic equilibrium because the duration of the combined
wave from the maximum extrema to the final amplitude is
approximately 32μs. Taken together, the wave comparison and
time duration of the wave to the 3t and 6t durations, dynamic
equilibrium should be present in the later portions of the data.

2.9 Stress and Strain Data Filtering
A tuned axis-symmetric Kolsky bar model grants the
ability to process results and create a stress-strain plot of the
sample. Using a low-pass data filter to process the results will
remove lingering inertial effects and possible contact errors
from the simulation, with a focus on removing impacts from
signal aliasing. An Infinite Impulse Response (IIR) filter
removed any possible jitter from the stress and strain results by
first applying an analog zero-phase filter. The analog zerophase filter does not contain any filter delay and removes any
possible phase distortion that an IIR filter is unstable to. A
Butterworth IIR filter was used to complete the filtering task
because of zero gain and slow roll-off capabilities to retain as
much data as possible while presenting no error margins for the
wave. Before the filter was placed on the data set, the 2 MHz

8

Copyright © 2024 by The United States Government

Downloaded from http://asmedigitalcollection.asme.org/IMECE/proceedings-pdf/IMECE2024/88681/V010T12A011/7428008/v010t12a011-imece2024-145918.pdf by The Pennsylvania State University user on 26 June 2025

sampling rate data was interpolated to a sampling data set of
200 MHz to allow a smooth waveform from the filtered data. A
cutoff frequency of 510 kHz was chosen to conserve data and
retain the 2 MHz sinusoidal input by preserving at least 4 points
of data per oscillation [6]. The Butterworth filter also required a
specific order fit prescribed by the user. From completing a
convergence study to understand which order fit would be
appropriate for the results, an order of four for matching the
larger magnitudes seen in the waveform. An order fit of three is
also a possible good fit for the filter, but a fourth order fit
retains the initial wave better than the third order fit.
Using the filtered data and material constants, an
estimation of the stress and strain of the sample is found from
the locations of the strain gauges on the incident and
transmission bars. An average of the strain output is taken from
the elements at the strain gauge location to receive an accurate
output. Transforming the data received from the strain gauges,
the elastic wave speed of the impact bars is required. The
equation:

cycle is approximately 2.649μs, and three to six wave cycles
are 7.947μs and 15.895μs respectively.

Figure 18: Sample’s stress-strain curve with identification of
the yield stress with an orange dotted vertical line, three wave
cycles with a green dot, and six wave cycles with a red dot.

In Figure 18, the orange dotted vertical line represents
the equivalent strain at the yield stress, the green dot represents
the beginning of possible dynamic equilibrium at three wave
cycles, and the red dot represents absolute dynamic equilibrium
at six wave cycles. Overall, the sample reaches a total strain of
0.0935 m/m, and an equivalent plastic strain of 0.0379 m/m as
seen from the simulation. The sample also experiences a
maximum stress of about 338 MPa.
3. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION
PMMA is extremely strain rate dependent as seen by the
data from W. R. Blumenthal et al. (4). The plastic region’s slope
increases as the strain rate increases and makes it difficult for an
accurate representation of plasticity and damage of a PMMA
sample to be replicated from data of a reference strain rate of
5s-1. Because of this, the resultant stress from strain of PMMA
samples in plasticity is incorrect in the JC model. For simulating
high strain rates using the JC model, only the end result, the
ultimate compression stress of the material, is accurate as seen
by Figure 7. While the resultant stress is inaccurate, the JC model
obtains the ultimate compression strength at the same strain
value as the experimental data. Given this outcome, the ultimate
compression strength of PMMA can be determined by the JC
plasticity model. The equivalent stress of the JC damage model
does not follow the experimental data and should present some
caution when accepting results past the ultimate compression
strength. The JC damage model is also seen to predict a fracture
approximately 0.005 mm/mm sooner than the experimental data.
From these results, experimental Kolsky bar data can be
reproduced given a correct statistical analysis of a large
population of results from a properly setup Kolsky bar system
such as presented in Hannah et al. (6).
The JC Plasticity model was implemented into a Kolsky
bar simulation to visualize and estimate the results from
experiments using Penn State’s Kolsky bar system. Tuning the
mesh of the sample, incident, and transmission bars increased the
model’s dependability and produced results will be used tom

4. CONCLUSION
In this paper, published research of Kolsky bar
experiments on PMMA was used to generate a JC plasticity and
damage model for an Abaqus® FE simulation of Penn State’s
Kolsky bar system. Using a single element static general
simulation, the JC plasticity model was proved to reproduceable
within FE simulation for a Kolsky bar. Given the single element
simulation’s result, an explicit dynamic simulation of a full
Kolsky bar system was created in Abaqus®. This model
simulates an experiment of Penn State’s Kolsky bar. A mesh
convergence study was conducted to determine the best
simulation parameters while conversing computing power. The
elastic sample mesh convergence study found a mesh of 50 by
50 elements was converged, while the plastic mesh convergence
study did not fully converge to a mesh density. To ensure the best
results, the converged elastic mesh is kept for producing results
to compare to our experimental results. For creating results with
higher accuracy, a mesh convergence study was conducted on

9

Copyright © 2024 by The United States Government

Downloaded from http://asmedigitalcollection.asme.org/IMECE/proceedings-pdf/IMECE2024/88681/V010T12A011/7428008/v010t12a011-imece2024-145918.pdf by The Pennsylvania State University user on 26 June 2025

compare to experimental data once it is generated. Any
imperfections with physical experimentation results will be
removed using the Butterworth IIR filter for an accurate stressstrain graph of the sample. With matching incident and combined
waveforms, and a guarantee of dynamic equilibrium for a
significant portion of the experiment, the axis-symmetric
simulation has proven to be a useful tool in understanding Penn
State’s Kolsky bar system and predicting its behavior.
The Kolsky bar experiments at Penn State require a
greater attention to detail over other commonly used Kolsky bar
experiments due to the diameter of the bars. Because smaller bars
are more sensitive, as determined by Hannah et al. (7), the results
of the Kolsky bar experiments may have a larger standard
deviation from what should be seen by the JC plasticity and
damage model. While increased sensitivity in the Kolsky bar
experiments may produce some variability in results, performing
tests on a large population of experiments should ultimately
reproduce the what should be estimated by the JC plasticity and
damage model.
With smaller diameter bars, higher strain rate
experiments can be tested to replicate extreme conditions on
various materials. More ductile materials than PMMA can be
used for Kolsky bar experiments to determine their reactions at
higher strain rates. Determining the material behavior of ductile
materials at high strain rates benefits the biomedical engineering
field to help deduce the reaction and effect of impacts on the
human body. Given a validated and verified Kolsky bar system
for usage with ductile materials, data for concussion induction
and the extent collision injuries can be predicted.
Further research for this study would include testing
PMMA samples at higher strain rates to determine if the JC
plasticity and damage models still can reproduce experimental
data. Dependent on the result, an investigation of using strain rate
data greater than 5s-1 and an investigation of what changes to the
JC plasticity and or damage model will be necessary to
accurately reproduce experimental results of ductile materials
given quasi-static strain rate data.

RESEARCH PAPER

High Speed Impact Testing of UHMWPE Composite Using
Orthogonal Arrays
T. Hannah1

· V. Martin1 · S. Ellis2 · R. H. Kraft3,4

Received: 7 July 2023 / Accepted: 25 March 2024 / Published online: 17 April 2024
© The Author(s) 2024

Abstract
Background Ultra high molecular weight polyethylene composites are fiber based composites used in armor applications.
While some characterization has been conducted experimentally, this study varies multiple parameters simultaneously
to investigate material response under a wide range of conditions.
Objective This work focuses on characterizing the response of ­Dyneema® HB26 hard laminate composites under high-speed
impacts to examine the influence of plate diameter, clamping pressure, and plate spacing on target performance. Additionally,
micro Computer Tomography scans are used to nondestructively evaluate the damage evolution in the targets.
Methods These scan results are used in concert with more traditional armor performance metrics to evaluate the effect of
various parameters using the method of orthogonal array analysis. This technique allows for multiple variables to be investigated in the same test series, saving time and budget while still providing quality results across a range of variables and
variable values.
Results We conclude that of the parameters investigated, the plate spacing parameter has the largest effect on performance,
followed by the plate diameter. Bolt torque was found to not have a significant impact on results, indicating that an edge
clamping pressure is not critical to material response. Additionally, by examining the high resolution scans, we can quantify
the damage with an effective damage angle and that this angle is a good predictor of performance.
Conclusion Finally a damage theory involving the effective bending strength of the plates is discussed as an explanation for
all of the results observed in this test series.
Keywords Composites · Orthogonal array testing · CT scanning · Composite failure · Impact testing

Introduction
Ultra-high molecular weight polyethylene (UHMWPE)
composites are fiber based polymer composites which have
grown in popularity as bullet and fragment mitigating armor
packages [1–6]. While there has been some experimentation conducted to characterize the responses of the laminate

little work has been done to characterize additional effects
such as the impact of various boundary conditions or the
effectiveness of multi-plate stacks of the composite relative to a consolidated whole, especially so in a single study
[8, 9]. The exact use case of the material, especially when
used for light vehicle protection, can include variations in

deployed configurations involving geometry changes and
boundary conditions based on the available volume and
protection implementation. These effects have the potential
to change the performance of the material, so understanding
the relative impact of them becomes critical. In this work,
HB26 ­Dyneema® panels are tested in multiple configurations using the method of orthogonal arrays for test design
[10, 11]. This testing structure allows for direct comparisons between a range of configurations including the influence of air gaps, the diameter of the targets, and clamping
pressure or in plane boundary conditions. High resolution
computer tomography (CT) methods are also employed to
non-destructively investigate the interior of targets post test
[9, 12]. The CT scans are used to evaluate the change in
damage mechanisms across the testing parameters, and a
new evaluation metric called the damage angle is proposed
as a new metric to quantify the internal damage effected
zone of the composite.

Methods and Setup
Test Matrix and Parameter Definitions
The test matrix used in the main battery of tests is shown in
Table 1. This testing scheme follows the method of orthogonal arrays, discussed in Appendix A, and reduces from 27
tests to the 9 shown here. The major evaluation criteria will
be exit velocity of the penetrator or amount of un-penetrated
material ahead of the projectile when there is no full penetration. The target projectile velocity, 1,030 m/s, should
be stopped by the 25.4 mm thick plates by design to give
an immediate result of performance if there is a penetration
of the multi-plate stacks. All of the 25.4 mm thick plates
stopped the projectile while all of the multi-plate stacks did
not, which helps simplify the analysis in some respects. This
will be further addressed in the “Results” section.

The parameters were selected to probe various responses
of the material. The plate diameter parameter and bolt torque
parameters investigate the impact of the boundary conditions
on performance. The diameter relates to the distance from
the impact location to the support, described in “Test Fixture Design” section and Appendix B, while the bolt torque
variations simulate the effect of semi-free vs fix boundary
conditions parallel to the plate face. The spacing parameter
is the most novel, weighing the potential benefit of allowing
deformation and failure intermittently vs load transmission
through the material via the transverse sound speed to spread
the load through the material. For the purposes of this study,
the gap is held constant for the two and four plate stacks with
the gap values based on preliminary testing results. Further
details on these parameters and the selection rationale are
provided in Appendix A.

Test Fixture Design
Each plate is clamped in a set of aluminum rings which are
then mounted to a larger testing frame which is aligned parallel to the axis of the barrel firing the projectile. The alignment is maintained by securing the frame to a rail which is
aligned and permanently affixed to the barrel mount. The
clamp ring assembly and mounting structure are shown in
Fig. 1 and full drawings are provided in Appendix B. Each
set of rings is designed such that the outer 16 mm of the
plate is secured between them. This leaves an exposed area
of 22.2 cm, 17.1 cm, and 12 cm for the three sized plates.
Each set of rings is secured together with 12 bolts which are
torqued down to the various prescribed values provided in
Table 1. In this structure, the gap between any two plates can
be set by moving the clamping rings to a desired location
and tightening the mounting screws to secure the plate in
place. For the 4 plate stacks, the plates are separated by the
rings shown on the third page of Appendix B. The assembly
is held together by threading bolts through the holes of each
ring and threading into the threaded back ring on the second
page of Appendix B. This configuration ensures that the gap
for each plate in these multi-plate stacks is consistent.

CT Scans
After the experiments are completed, the plates are scanned
using a micro CT scanner capable of scanning at voxel resolutions up to 1 um. For these samples, an area of approximately 10 cm by 10 cm was scanned at a resolution of 55.7
um. This provided a good compromise between amount of
plate scanned vs resolution for the machine. While this is
not fine enough to resolve individual fibers, which measure
17-19 um, it is enough to examine plys and evaluate failure
mechanisms such as delamination, fiber breakage, and other
meso and macro mechanical mechanisms. To achieve this

Experimental Mechanics (2024) 64:823–838

825

Fig. 1  The plates are clamped between aluminum rings and mounted to a rigid test frame as shown on the left, with potential mount points on
the top, bottom, left, and right. The image on the right shows how multiple plates can be inserted into the test frame and gaps can be prescribed
at the user’s discretion. The full schematics are given in Appendix B

level of resolution the 25.4 cm diameter targets needed to
be cut down to 20.3 cm. This is to ensure that the sensor
head of the CT could get close enough to the area of interest
to maintain spatial resolution. The 25.4 cm samples were
cut using a waterjetting process in which the samples were
clamped between two sacrificial aluminum rings. The rings
both added compressive strength to prevent layer blow-out
and reduced the harshness of the cut on the composite by
having the initial blast from the jet interact with the sacrificial metal surface first. This resulted in a smooth cut surface
with minimal aggregate deposition and no added damage in
the specimens. Practice cuts on spare stock were conducted
and no delamination or other defects were observed after
the cutting. Little to no aggregate or other particulates were
seen in the CT scans of these plates, providing additional
confidence that the area of interest around the penetration
sight was not affected by the cutting process.

Results and Discussion
Calibration Tests
Before the actual experimental specifications were finalized, several calibration shots were run to gather initial data
and roughly determine the impact speed to be used for the
main series of tests. In these calibration tests it was found
that the approximate penetration velocity for the 9.5 mm
diameter hardened tool steel projectile was 1100 m/s for the
20.3 cm by 2.54 cm target which was taken as the baseline.

During these tests a 15.2 cm by 2.54 cm plate was able to
stop a projectile with an initial velocity of 1170 m/s, giving
a preliminary insight into the impact of diameter on material
behavior being that a smaller diameter leads to increased
ballistic performance. From these tests, a target velocity of
1,025–1,035 m/s was selected for the main testing group.
This selection helped get an immediate result of the change
in performance if the multi-plate stacks were fully penetrated, which indeed they were.

Main Testing Results
The results of the main testing array are shown below in
Table 2. All of the consolidated 25.4 mm thick plates stopped
the projectile inside the plate. The 15.2 cm diameter plates
performed the best with 4.64 mm of unpunctured plate remaining ahead of the projectile, a 53% improvement over the largest
25.4 cm diameter plate of the same thickness and about 14%
better than the 20.3 cm diameter plate. While each test did
have different bolt torque and therefore clamp pressure, this
preliminary assessment aligns with the conclusions from the
calibration testing which shows that smaller diameter plates
perform better when in this type of testing configuration. Such
a result indicates that the interactions with the plate and assembly frame lead to increased performance, as the smaller diameter plate is able to transfer some of the load into the frame
faster via the wave speed of the plate compared to larger plates
which have a longer travel distance to the assembly frame.
The smaller plates also perform better in the multi plate tests,
as tests 2 and 5 have two of the lowest exit velocities of the

two and four plate stacks respectively with 345 m/s for the
two plate and 310 m/s for the four plate configurations. Test 4
involving two 20.3 cm plates did have the lowest exit velocity,
but it also had the lowest initial velocity by about 20 m/s compared to the next lowest and 35 m/s below the target velocity
which should in part account for the perceived increased performance. This decrease is due to the projectile slightly nicking
the sabot catcher on the way to the target, which slightly altered
its trajectory and decreased its impact velocity. However, the
impact velocity is still high enough to consider this data point
with the caveat that there may be more error introduced by the
slight angle of the terminal flight path for that test and slightly
lower velocity. This will be considered in detail when evaluating the results from the experimental series.
Orthogonal array results
The orthogonal array analysis is complicated slightly by
the fact that the single 25.4 mm thick plate tests of every
diameter stopped the projectile, while the rest did not. This
means that no one continuous parameter can be used for
evaluation in the method via standard means. We therefore
make a slight adjustment to the method in that when evaluating the plate diameter and bolt torque parameters, only the
tests from which an exit velocity is measured are used for
effectiveness calculations. The results from the single plate
tests will be evaluated separately, and connections will be
made relating the single plate tests to the results from the
level averages study. Additionally, a parameter called damage angle is used as a continuous parameter in this evaluation. This parameter is found by processing the CT images
and determining the angle separating the little to no damage zone from the area of significant damage. The angle is
determined by a visual inspection process of the CT images
using the software package AVIZO and identifying the point
of first far field and major delamination. The flat front face
of the plate far from the disturbed location of penetration
is used as the horizontal reference for the damage angle.
The vertex is placed just before the main damage channel

where the density of material drops significantly. The angle
is then measured from this horizontal reference to the major
delamination described previously. This process can involve
some error as the scans are accurate to 55.7 um and a visual
identification of the major delamination site is used, with
total process error estimated at ± 0.05 degrees. This is not
enough to impact the results of this study, and allows for
the use of another quantifiable parameter for evaluation of
results and provide value to the community. The 2.54 cm by
15.2 cm plate scan is shown in Fig. 2 as an example of the
CT scan an angle identification. The average damage angle
is shown to be 18.5 degrees and it is seen that the significant
observed damage occurs above the line of the angle, while
mostly consolidated and undamaged material is underneath
it. This observation and pattern hold for all plates as will be
seen in scans shown in future sections. The damage patterns
lend themselves to this type of linear progression of damage
from the impact location and is consistent with the observed
tenting effect of Dyneema during high-speed impacts [9, 13,
14]. All images are therefore taken when the scan result is
aligned with one of the fiber directions such that a 0° layer
is parallel running left/right in plane and a 90° layer is perpendicular out of the plane.
Plate Spacing The results from the level average study for
plate spacing is shown in Fig. 3. This clearly indicates that
a single plate performs best, and that continuing to spread
out the material while maintaining areal density decreases
performance. Test 4 is included in the 2 × 12.7 mm category,
but the trend of the two plate test performing better than the
four plate tests is consistent when looking at the 25.4 cm
diameter tests indicating a minimal impact on the conclusion of the analysis. Curiously, it does not appear that the
15.2 cm plates are as sensitive to the plate spacing, with the
two and four plate configurations at that diameter behaving
effectively the same. This claim is re-affirmed by the even
closer matching delta v values for the two tests and the fact
that the higher exit velocity of the pair also has the slightly
higher input velocity. Additionally, the difference in bolt

with the exit velocity criteria which validates the use of damage angle for material performance. The CT scan results for
the 20.3 cm diameter plates, shown in Fig. 4, illustrate the
decrease in the damage angle with an increase in plate number. The highest average damage angle for a given diameter is
always on the 1 × 25.4 mm test, followed by the 2 × 12.7 and
finally the 4 × 6.35. For the 2 plate tests, the first plate always
has a better damage angle compared to the second, with both
plates being worse than the single plate tests. The second plate
damage angle is also always greater than any damage angle
for any plate from the 4 plate tests for a given diameter. These
higher damage angles in the 2 plate tests help explain why the
2 plate tests perform better than the 4 plates when considering
the exit velocity as an evaluation metric. To approximate this
trend numerically, the damage angle can be predicted with the
following equation:

damage angle = 20.511 − 4.3083 (number of plates)
This equation has a strong resemblance to the experimentally collected data with an R squared value of 1 indicating
not only an excellent fit, but also a strong linearity in the
relationship between the number plates and damage angle.
Curiously, the third plate in the 4 plate stacks always has the
greatest damage angle; 7.4, 9.3 and 11.7 degrees corresponding
to the 25.4, 20.3, and 15.2 cm diameter cases. This increase in
damage angle and therefore performance in a later plate is likely
due to the slowing of the projectile and an interaction between
the plates. The second plate is deforming and making contact
with the third plate, pre-loading it before the actual penetration
event, and the third plate is also similarly interacting with the
final plate which provides some degree of reinforcement and
therefore and increase in performance.

Plate Diameter Results for analyzing plate diameter are shown
in Fig. 5 which indicate that performance decreases with an
increase in plate diameter. Again, these values are calculated
using only the non-zero exit velocities from the two and four
plate tests. These results are however supported by the CT scans
of the single plate tests which shows the same trend in amount
of unpenetrated plate, as seen in Table 2 and Fig. 6, meaning
that the smallest diameter plate had the best performance. This
is consistent with the findings presented in Fig. 5. The slightly
anomalous Test 4 is included in the 20.3 cm plate calculations,
but qualitatively accounting for this would result in a slight
increase in the average exit velocity for the 20.3 cm series which
would not alter the conclusions of the analysis.
When analyzing the CT images and damage angle a
similar trend is observed where the smallest diameter has
the largest angle which can be seen in Fig. 6. Along with
these values the distance from the front of the plate to the
first major delamination, as noted by the yellow lines in the
bottom left of each image of Fig. 6, also increase as the
diameter decreases. Also of interest is the clear change in
failure mechanism with the diameter. As the plate diameter
is reduced there is significant cavitation, or creation of large
pockets of empty space in the material, and large layer separation is generated. This leads to a larger back-face deformation and an increase in performance, which is counter to
conventional methods when evaluating body armor which
rank plates lower when the back-face deformation is larger
[15, 16]. All three plates at the 25.4 mm thickness show significant fiber curling at the transition from shear plug failure
to significant load in the fibers and fiber snap, which coincides with the depth of the first major delamination and is
circled in red. To numerically quantify the damage angle as a
function of plate diameter, a linear relationship is defined as:

damage angle = 12.776 − 1.3643 (plate diameter in cm)
This equation on its own does a fair job of capturing
the trend with an R squared value of 0.86. Logically, there
must be a point of diminishing returns on either extreme for
diameter where at a very large diameter the damage angle
approaches a fixed value and at some smaller diameter the
angle (and performance) must decrease as there will be too
little material to effectively absorb and / or dissipate the
energy from the impact event. However, over this range of
diameters the fit of this equation is applicable and shows a
good correlation to collected data.
Bolt Torque Bolt torque analysis results are shown in Fig. 7.
The analysis seems to indicate that the material would perform better at either very high or very low bolt torques, but
the low torque spec of 2.7 N-m contains the data point from
the slightly anomalous test 4 which could be decreasing the
average exit velocity in this analysis. Further, evaluating
the single 25.4 mm thick test shots shows that the medium
torque parameter behaves best followed by the high spec
with the low torque performing the worst. When considering
these results together, we conclude that the clamping pressure does not have a strong trend although that it appears as
though higher bolt torques may be beneficial. This is likely
caused by the compressive preload of the higher bolt torques

closing any meso or microscopic voids in the material thus
increasing the overall strength by facilitating better stress
wave transmission through the entire target.
The trendline in damage angle vs bolt torque is nearly flat
as shown in Fig. 7, and the R squared value is less than 0.01.
This finding again confirms that the bolt torque has minimal effect on impact tests of this type. Additionally, there is
nothing in the CT scans of the similar bolt torque level tests
that consistently links them across tests further confirming
the assertion stated previously.

CT Scan Analysis and Failure Mechanism Discussion
All targets display a delamination / damage angle which
shows that the damage grows in plane as the projectile penetrates. The damage angle was discussed in the analysis of
"Orthogonal array results" section, so the focus of this section is to examine the other failure mechanisms observed in
the CT scans. For the purpose of brevity, the failure mechanisms discussion is organized by the plate spacing parameter as that had the strongest trend when looking at both the
residual projectile velocity and the damage angle as evaluation parameters. On the whole, increased delamination based
cavitation is the primary mechanism that leads to increased
performance, as across all three plate spacing categories
those with increased performance displayed increased cavity generation. This effect is not as strong in the 4 × 6.35 mm

25.4 mm thick plates
The scans of the 25.4 mm thick targets show a considerable
change in failure mechanism and are displayed in Fig. 6.
While the amount of un-penetrated plate remaining increases
with a decreasing diameter, the total back face deformation
appears to have the inverse relationship. This likely leads to
the apparent increase in performance for smaller diameter
plates as more delamination occurs and more load is taken
up in the fibers due to the indirect tensile mechanism having
more of an opportunity to take effect. Additionally, the larger
diameter 25.4 cm target does not have as clear of a division
between shear plug and delamination zones.
The overall trend among the tests in Fig. 6 indicates
that peak back face deformation is in fact linked to the
damage angle of the target, and that an increase in either
is indicative of increased performance when evaluating
based upon amount of un-penetrated plate remaining.
This is again consistent with the discussion in "Calibration Tests" section in which the 25.4 mm × 15.2 cm target
was able to capture a 1170 m/s projectile, which is 70 m/s
faster than the penetration speed of a 20.3 cm diameter
target. After the shear plug failure zone, which is the initial
damage zone with little to no delaminations far from the
penetration channel, all of the targets show a significant
curl in the fibers. This curl, referred to as fiber snap back,
is a key internal failure feature and exists at the transition from shear plug to delamination damage dominated
failure. This transition occurs deeper into the targets as
the diameter decreases as noted by the depth of the first
minor far field delamination shown in the lower left of the
images in Fig. 6. The variation is only one millimeter in
